Correctly: $$\mathbb{E}[T] = 0.6\cdot\frac{2}{5} + 0.4\cdot\frac{2}{30} = 0.6(0.4) + 0.4(0.0666\overline{6}) = 0.24 + 0.0266\overline{6} = \frac{4}{15} \approx 0.2667\ \text{hours} = 16\ \text{minutes}.$$ Incorrectly averaging the speed first: $\mathbb{E}[V] = 0.6(5)+0.4(30) = 3+12 = 15$ mph, then $2/\mathbb{E}[V] = 2/15 \approx 0.1333$ hours $= 8$ minutes — off by a factor of exactly two. Averaging inside a nonlinear function is not the same as averaging outside it (keys alice_*). The one safe case is a linear $g$, which is the subject of §2.3.
    Interpretation. The expected value rule says something stronger than "here is a shortcut". It says that a mean is a property of the mass, not of the labels: to average $g(X)$ you never need to know how $g$ collapses different $x$'s onto the same $y$ — you only need to walk the values of $X$ once, carrying $g(x)$ along. The "hard" route rebuilds a whole PMF just to throw all but one number away. Two consequences worth holding on to. (i) Every moment of $X$ — $\mathbb{E}[X]$, $\mathbb{E}[X^2]$, $\operatorname{var}(X)$, and the whole of §2.3 — is a single instance of this one rule with a different $g$. (ii) The rule is the exact reason $\mathbb{E}[g(X)]$ and $g(\mathbb{E}[X])$ differ: the rule applies $g$ before the weighted sum, and for nonlinear $g$ the order matters, since $g$ then bends the spacing between values and so re-weights the torques of Fig. 2.1. Linear $g$ is precisely the class that preserves that spacing — which is why §2.3 exists as a separate, easy story.
    Practice 2.3. Let $X$ be uniform on $\{0,1,2,3\}$ and let $g(x) = 1/(x+1)$. Compute $\mathbb{E}[g(X)]$ and $g(\mathbb{E}[X])$, and say which is larger.
    Solution

    By the expected value rule, with $p_X(k) = 1/4$ for $k=0,1,2,3$: $$\mathbb{E}\!\left[\frac{1}{X+1}\right] = \frac{1}{1}\cdot\frac14 + \frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac14 + \frac{1}{3}\cdot\frac14 + \frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac14 = \frac14\Big(1 + \tfrac12 + \tfrac13 + \tfrac14\Big) = \frac14\cdot\frac{12+6+4+3}{12} = \frac{25}{48} \approx 0.520833 .$$ Meanwhile $\mathbb{E}[X] = \frac{0+1+2+3}{4} = \frac32 = 1.5$, so $$g(\mathbb{E}[X]) = \frac{1}{1.5+1} = \frac{1}{2.5} = \frac{2}{5} = 0.4 .$$ So $\mathbb{E}[g(X)] = 0.5208 > 0.4 = g(\mathbb{E}[X])$ (keys pq_recip_E, pq_recip_g_of_EX). The gap is in this direction because $g(x)=1/(x+1)$ is convex on $x\ge 0$ — a fact that gets a name (Jensen's inequality) later; for now the point is only that the two quantities differ.

    Practice 2.4. $X$ has $p_X(-1) = 0.5$, $p_X(0)=0.2$, $p_X(1)=0.3$. Let $Y = |X|$. Find $\mathbb{E}[Y]$ by both routes.
    Solution

    Easy: $\mathbb{E}[|X|] = |-1|(0.5) + |0|(0.2) + |1|(0.3) = 0.5 + 0 + 0.3 = 0.8$.

    Hard: $p_Y(0) = p_X(0) = 0.2$ and $p_Y(1) = p_X(-1)+p_X(1) = 0.5+0.3 = 0.8$ (both $x=\pm1$ map to $y=1$; add their masses by $(\ast)$). Then $\mathbb{E}[Y] = 0(0.2) + 1(0.8) = 0.8$. ✓

    Note $\mathbb{E}[X] = -0.5+0+0.3 = -0.2$, so $|\mathbb{E}[X]| = 0.2 \neq 0.8$: absolute value is nonlinear, so the rule of §2.3 does not apply to it.

    2.3 Linearity: the three blanks on L05 slide 7

    Slide 7 poses three fill-in-the-blank identities for constants $\alpha,\beta$ and prints no answers. Each is a one-line consequence of the expected value rule, and together they are the single most-used fact in the course.

    Property (L05 slide 7 — the three blanks, filled). For any random variable $X$ and any constants $\alpha,\beta$: $$\mathbb{E}[\alpha] = \alpha, \qquad \mathbb{E}[\alpha X] = \alpha\,\mathbb{E}[X], \qquad \mathbb{E}[\alpha X + \beta] = \alpha\,\mathbb{E}[X] + \beta .$$

    Blank 1: $\mathbb{E}[\alpha] = \alpha$. Here $\alpha$ means the constant random variable $g(X) = \alpha$ — it takes the value $\alpha$ whatever the outcome. By the expected value rule with $g(x)\equiv\alpha$, $$\mathbb{E}[\alpha] = \sum_x \alpha\,p_X(x) = \alpha \sum_x p_X(x) = \alpha \cdot 1 = \alpha,$$ where the last step uses the normalization property $\sum_x p_X(x)=1$ from L05 slide 3. Interpretation: something that is never random averages to itself.

    Blank 2: $\mathbb{E}[\alpha X] = \alpha\mathbb{E}[X]$. Take $g(x) = \alpha x$: $$\mathbb{E}[\alpha X] = \sum_x \alpha x\,p_X(x) = \alpha \sum_x x\,p_X(x) = \alpha\,\mathbb{E}[X],$$ pulling the constant $\alpha$ out of the sum (distributivity) and recognizing the definition of $\mathbb{E}[X]$. Interpretation: change the units of $X$ (dollars to cents, $\alpha = 100$) and the mean changes units the same way.

    Blank 3: $\mathbb{E}[\alpha X + \beta] = \alpha\mathbb{E}[X] + \beta$. Take $g(x) = \alpha x + \beta$ and split the sum: $$\mathbb{E}[\alpha X + \beta] = \sum_x (\alpha x + \beta)\,p_X(x) = \underbrace{\sum_x \alpha x\,p_X(x)}_{=\ \alpha\mathbb{E}[X]\ \text{by Blank 2}} + \underbrace{\sum_x \beta\,p_X(x)}_{=\ \beta\ \text{by Blank 1}} = \alpha\,\mathbb{E}[X] + \beta .$$ This is exactly the derivation on B&T p. 86 that rec05 P1b defers to. Note where each hypothesis is used: splitting the sum needs only additivity, but the second piece collapses to $\beta$ only because the masses total $1$. Numerical audit: for $X$ uniform on $\{-2,\dots,2\}$ ($\mathbb{E}[X]=0$) with $\alpha=3$, $\beta=-7$, direct summation gives $\mathbb{E}[3X-7] = -7$ and the formula gives $3(0)-7 = -7$ ✓ (keys lin_direct, lin_formula).

    Example 2.3 (rec05 P1 in full — all three derivations the official solution defers to the textbook). The recitation asks: (a) derive the expected value rule; (b) derive $\mathbb{E}[Y] = a\mathbb{E}[X]+b$ and $\operatorname{var}(Y) = a^2\operatorname{var}(X)$ for $Y = aX+b$; (c) derive $\operatorname{var}(X) = \mathbb{E}[X^2] - (\mathbb{E}[X])^2$. The official solution reads, in its entirety: "(a) See derivation in textbook pp. 84–85. (b) See derivation in textbook p. 86. (c) See derivation in textbook p. 87."
    Solution

    (a) Done above, in §2.2 — the grouping argument from $p_Y(y) = \sum_{\{x:g(x)=y\}} p_X(x)$ to $\mathbb{E}[g(X)] = \sum_x g(x)p_X(x)$.

    (b) Mean. Done just above (Blank 3), with $\alpha = a$, $\beta = b$: $$\mathbb{E}[aX+b] = \sum_x (ax+b)p_X(x) = a\sum_x x\,p_X(x) + b\sum_x p_X(x) = a\,\mathbb{E}[X] + b .$$

    (b) Variance. Recall $\operatorname{var}(Z) = \mathbb{E}\big[(Z - \mathbb{E}[Z])^2\big]$ (L05 slide 8; §3 of this note develops variance properly). Put $Y = aX+b$ and use the expected value rule with $g(x) = (ax+b-\mathbb{E}[Y])^2$: $$\operatorname{var}(Y) = \sum_x \big(ax + b - \mathbb{E}[aX+b]\big)^2 p_X(x).$$ Substitute $\mathbb{E}[aX+b] = a\mathbb{E}[X]+b$ from the first half, and watch the $b$'s cancel: $$ax + b - \big(a\mathbb{E}[X] + b\big) = ax - a\mathbb{E}[X] = a\big(x - \mathbb{E}[X]\big).$$ Therefore $$\operatorname{var}(Y) = \sum_x a^2\big(x - \mathbb{E}[X]\big)^2 p_X(x) = a^2 \sum_x \big(x-\mathbb{E}[X]\big)^2 p_X(x) = a^2 \operatorname{var}(X),$$ squaring the factor $a$ out of the bracket and then pulling the constant $a^2$ out of the sum. Two readings: an additive shift $b$ does not change spread (it moves the whole picture), and a scaling by $a$ multiplies spread by $a^2$ — hence by $|a|$ in standard-deviation units.

    (c) The variance identity. Start from the definition and expand the square. Write $\mu = \mathbb{E}[X]$ for brevity — a fixed number, not a random quantity: $$\begin{aligned} \operatorname{var}(X) &= \sum_x (x-\mu)^2 p_X(x) &&\text{expected value rule with } g(x)=(x-\mu)^2\\ &= \sum_x \big(x^2 - 2\mu x + \mu^2\big) p_X(x) &&\text{algebraic expansion of the square}\\ &= \sum_x x^2 p_X(x) \;-\; 2\mu\sum_x x\,p_X(x) \;+\; \mu^2\sum_x p_X(x) &&\text{split into three sums; } \mu \text{ is constant}\\ &= \mathbb{E}[X^2] \;-\; 2\mu\cdot\mu \;+\; \mu^2 \cdot 1 &&\text{definitions of } \mathbb{E}[X^2],\ \mathbb{E}[X],\ \text{and normalization}\\ &= \mathbb{E}[X^2] - 2\mu^2 + \mu^2 \;=\; \mathbb{E}[X^2] - \mu^2 \;=\; \mathbb{E}[X^2] - \big(\mathbb{E}[X]\big)^2 . \end{aligned}$$ Rearranged, this is the form used constantly for second moments: $$\boxed{\ \mathbb{E}[X^2] = \operatorname{var}(X) + \big(\mathbb{E}[X]\big)^2\ }$$ which is how rec05 P2(f) gets $\mathbb{E}[X^2]$ from two numbers already computed. Note also that since $\operatorname{var}(X)\ge 0$ (it is a sum of nonnegative terms), the identity forces $\mathbb{E}[X^2] \ge (\mathbb{E}[X])^2$ — the second moment is always at least the square of the mean, with equality only if $X$ is constant.

    Recipe (computing $\mathbb{E}[g(X)]$).
    1. Is $g$ linear, i.e. $g(x) = \alpha x + \beta$? Then stop thinking and write $\mathbb{E}[g(X)] = \alpha\mathbb{E}[X]+\beta$. No sum needed. (Special cases: $g(x)=x+\beta$ shifts the mean; $g(x)=\alpha x$ rescales it.)
    2. Otherwise, do you have $p_X$? Then use the expected value rule directly: tabulate $x$, $g(x)$, $p_X(x)$, multiply across, add the column. Never build $p_Y$.
    3. Is $g(x)=x^2$? Either sum $\sum_x x^2 p_X(x)$, or — if you already know the mean and variance — use $\mathbb{E}[X^2] = \operatorname{var}(X) + (\mathbb{E}[X])^2$.
    4. Only if you actually need the distribution of $Y$ (not just its mean) should you compute $p_Y(y) = \sum_{\{x:g(x)=y\}}p_X(x)$.
    5. Never write $\mathbb{E}[g(X)] = g(\mathbb{E}[X])$ for nonlinear $g$. Check step 1 before you are tempted.
    Fig. 2.2 — Decision guide for $\mathbb{E}[g(X)]$, assembled from L05 slide 7 ("hard"/"easy"/caution) and rec05 P1a. The left column is the cheap route; the right column is the route you take only when you genuinely need $p_Y$.
    Gotcha. Linearity of expectation is about a linear function of one random variable here — that is all L05 slide 7 claims. It is not a licence to write $\mathbb{E}[XY] = \mathbb{E}[X]\mathbb{E}[Y]$ (false in general; true under independence — see §4.5 for independence of random variables and §4.6 for the product rule), and the far stronger fact $\mathbb{E}[X+Y] = \mathbb{E}[X]+\mathbb{E}[Y]$ for arbitrary, even dependent, $X$ and $Y$ arrives with joint PMFs. Also note the asymmetry between mean and variance: $\mathbb{E}[aX+b] = a\mathbb{E}[X]+b$ keeps the $b$, whereas $\operatorname{var}(aX+b) = a^2\operatorname{var}(X)$ drops it and squares the $a$.
    Practice 2.5. A thermostat reading in Celsius has mean $\mathbb{E}[C] = 20$ and $\operatorname{var}(C) = 9$. Fahrenheit is $F = \frac95 C + 32$. Find $\mathbb{E}[F]$, $\operatorname{var}(F)$, and $\mathbb{E}[F^2]$.
    Solution

    $g$ is linear with $\alpha = 9/5 = 1.8$, $\beta = 32$, so $$\mathbb{E}[F] = \tfrac95(20) + 32 = 36 + 32 = 68\ ^\circ\text{F}, \qquad \operatorname{var}(F) = \big(\tfrac95\big)^2 (9) = \tfrac{81}{25}\cdot 9 = \tfrac{729}{25} = 29.16 .$$ The $+32$ shifts the mean but leaves the variance alone. Then by the identity of Example 2.3(c), $$\mathbb{E}[F^2] = \operatorname{var}(F) + (\mathbb{E}[F])^2 = 29.16 + 68^2 = 29.16 + 4624 = 4653.16 .$$ (Key pq_temp_EF $=68$.) Notice you could not have obtained $\mathbb{E}[F^2]$ as $(9/5)^2\mathbb{E}[C^2]$ — squaring is nonlinear, and the cross term matters.

    Practice 2.6. True or false, with a one-line reason: (i) $\mathbb{E}[3X] = 3\mathbb{E}[X]$; (ii) $\mathbb{E}[X^3] = (\mathbb{E}[X])^3$; (iii) $\operatorname{var}(X+7) = \operatorname{var}(X)+7$; (iv) $\mathbb{E}[X^2] \ge (\mathbb{E}[X])^2$ always.
    Solution

    (i) True — Blank 2 of L05 slide 7, $g(x)=3x$ is linear. (ii) False — cubing is nonlinear; e.g. $X = \pm 1$ with probability $1/2$ each gives $\mathbb{E}[X^3] = 0 = (\mathbb{E}[X])^3$ by accident, but $X$ uniform on $\{0,2\}$ gives $\mathbb{E}[X^3] = 4$ while $(\mathbb{E}[X])^3 = 1$. (iii) False — $\operatorname{var}(aX+b) = a^2\operatorname{var}(X)$ with $a=1,b=7$ gives $\operatorname{var}(X)$; adding a constant shifts, never spreads. (iv) True — rearranging Example 2.3(c), $\mathbb{E}[X^2]-(\mathbb{E}[X])^2 = \operatorname{var}(X) \ge 0$, with equality exactly when $X$ is constant.

    2.4 Worked recitation problem: the marksman (rec05 P2)

    Example 2.4 (rec05 P2). A marksman takes $10$ shots at a target and has probability $0.2$ of hitting the target with each shot, independently of all other shots. Let $X$ be the number of hits.
    (a) Calculate and sketch the PMF of $X$.
    (b) What is the probability of scoring no hits?
    (c) What is the probability of scoring more hits than misses?
    (d) Find the expectation and the variance of $X$.
    (e) Suppose the marksman has to pay $\$3$ to enter the shooting range and he gets $\$2$ for each hit. Let $Y$ be his profit. Find the expectation and the variance of $Y$.
    (f) Now assume the marksman enters for free and gets the number of dollars equal to the square of the number of hits. Let $Z$ be his profit. Find the expectation of $Z$.
    Solution

    (a) The model and the PMF. Each shot is an independent trial with two outcomes, hit (probability $p=0.2$) or miss (probability $1-p=0.8$), and $X$ counts the hits in $n=10$ trials. That is exactly the binomial setup of L05 slide 5, so $$p_X(k) = \binom{10}{k}(0.2)^k (0.8)^{10-k}, \qquad k = 0,1,\dots,10,$$ and $p_X(k)=0$ otherwise. Reading the formula: $(0.2)^k(0.8)^{10-k}$ is the probability of one specific sequence with $k$ hits and $10-k$ misses (multiply per-shot probabilities, by independence), and $\binom{10}{k}$ counts how many such sequences there are (choose which $k$ of the $10$ shots are hits). The numerical table (key mk_pmf_*; total $=1$ exactly):

    $k$012345678910
    $\binom{10}{k}$1104512021025221012045101
    $p_X(k)$0.107370.268440.301990.201330.088080.026420.0055050.0007867.37e-54.10e-61.02e-7

    The peak is at $k=2$, which here coincides with the mean $\mathbb{E}[X]=np=2$ computed in (d) — see Fig. 2.3, which reproduces the scatter sketch of the official solution with the numbers filled in.

    (b) No hits. Set $k=0$: $\binom{10}{0}=1$ and $(0.2)^0 = 1$, so $$\mathbf{P}(\text{no hits}) = p_X(0) = (0.8)^{10} = 0.1073741824 \approx \boxed{0.1074},$$ matching the official answer. (Directly: all ten shots must miss, and by independence the ten probabilities $0.8$ multiply.) It is worth noticing how large this is — a marksman who expects $2$ hits still comes away empty-handed about $11\%$ of the time.

    (c) More hits than misses. With $10$ shots, hits $=k$ and misses $=10-k$, so "more hits than misses" means $$k > 10 - k \iff 2k > 10 \iff k > 5 \iff k \ge 6 \quad (k \text{ is an integer}).$$ (At $k=5$ there are five of each — a tie, not "more", so $k=5$ is excluded.) The events $\{X=k\}$ are disjoint, so by additivity we add the five masses. The official solution prints only the total $0.0064$; here is the sum term by term (key mk_tail_terms): $$\begin{aligned} \mathbf{P}(X \ge 6) &= \textstyle\sum_{k=6}^{10}\binom{10}{k}(0.2)^k(0.8)^{10-k}\\ &= \underbrace{210(0.2)^6(0.8)^4}_{0.00550502} + \underbrace{120(0.2)^7(0.8)^3}_{0.00078643} + \underbrace{45(0.2)^8(0.8)^2}_{0.00007373} + \underbrace{10(0.2)^9(0.8)^1}_{0.00000410} + \underbrace{1(0.2)^{10}}_{0.00000010}\\ &= 0.0063693824 \;\approx\; \boxed{0.0064}. \ \checkmark \end{aligned}$$ The first term alone is $86\%$ of the total: the tail collapses geometrically, each further hit costing a factor of roughly $\frac{0.2}{0.8}=\frac14$ times a shrinking binomial coefficient.

    (d) Mean and variance. The official solution quotes the binomial formulas $\mathbb{E}[X]=np$ and $\operatorname{var}(X)=np(1-p)$ without derivation. Those formulas are proved cleanly once expectations of sums are available (write $X = X_1+\cdots+X_{10}$ with $X_i$ the indicator of a hit on shot $i$; that is §4.6 and §4.8 machinery). With only §2 tools we can still get the numbers honestly, straight from the definition (keys mk_EX_terms, mk_EX2_terms): $$\mathbb{E}[X] = \sum_{k=0}^{10} k\,p_X(k) = 0 + 0.268435 + 0.603980 + 0.603980 + 0.352322 + 0.132121 + 0.033030 + 0.005505 + 0.000590 + 0.000037 + 0.000001 = 2 .$$ The sum is exactly $2$, confirming $np = 10(0.2) = \boxed{2}$. For the variance, first the second moment by the expected value rule with $g(x)=x^2$: $$\mathbb{E}[X^2] = \sum_{k=0}^{10} k^2 p_X(k) = 0 + 0.268435 + 1.207960 + 1.811939 + 1.409286 + 0.660603 + 0.198181 + 0.038535 + 0.004719 + 0.000332 + 0.000010 = 5.6 ,$$ then the identity of Example 2.3(c): $$\operatorname{var}(X) = \mathbb{E}[X^2] - (\mathbb{E}[X])^2 = 5.6 - 2^2 = 5.6 - 4 = \boxed{1.6},$$ confirming $np(1-p) = 10(0.2)(0.8) = 1.6$. The standard deviation is $\sigma_X = \sqrt{1.6} = 1.2649$ hits.

    (e) Profit $Y = 2X - 3$. Each hit pays $\$2$ and the entry fee of $\$3$ is paid regardless, so the profit is a linear function of $X$ — exactly the case L05 slide 7 covers. With $\alpha = 2$, $\beta = -3$: $$\mathbb{E}[Y] = 2\,\mathbb{E}[X] - 3 = 2(2) - 3 = 4 - 3 = \boxed{1}\ \text{dollar},$$ $$\operatorname{var}(Y) = 2^2 \operatorname{var}(X) = 4(1.6) = \boxed{6.4}, \qquad \sigma_Y = \sqrt{6.4} = 2.5298\ \text{dollars}.$$ Cross-check by brute force, summing $(2k-3)p_X(k)$ over all $k$: the answer is again exactly $1$ (key mk_EY_direct). Note the fee shifts the mean profit but leaves the variance untouched, while the $\$2$-per-hit rate multiplies the standard deviation by $2$. Interpretation: on average the marksman makes a dollar, but with $\sigma_Y \approx \$2.53$ he loses money whenever $X \le 1$, which happens with probability $p_X(0)+p_X(1) = 0.1074 + 0.2684 = 0.3758$ — better than a one-in-three chance of walking away poorer.

    (f) Squared profit $Z = X^2$. Now $g$ is not linear, so $\mathbb{E}[Z] \ne (\mathbb{E}[X])^2 = 4$. Use the expected value rule; and rather than resumming, reuse the rearranged variance identity — the step the official solution performs silently: $$\mathbb{E}[Z] = \mathbb{E}[X^2] = \operatorname{var}(X) + \big(\mathbb{E}[X]\big)^2 = 1.6 + 2^2 = 1.6 + 4 = \boxed{5.6}\ \text{dollars},$$ in agreement with the direct sum $\sum_k k^2 p_X(k) = 5.6$ computed in (d). The gap between $5.6$ and the naive $4$ is precisely $\operatorname{var}(X) = 1.6$: paying for the square of the hit count rewards the marksman for his variability.

    Fig. 2.3 — PMF of the marksman's hit count, $X \sim \text{Binomial}(10,\,0.2)$ (rec05 P2a; the official solution sketches the same points as bare circles). The shaded region is the event "more hits than misses", $X \ge 6$, of probability $0.006369$ (part c); the fulcrum marks $\mathbb{E}[X]=2$ (part d). The right panel is a log-scale magnification showing that the five tail masses fall by roughly a factor of $7$–$40$ at each step.
    Interpretation — what parts (e) and (f) are really testing. The marksman problem is built so that its last two parts contrast, and the contrast is the point. Part (e) has a linear payout $Y = 2X-3$, and linearity lets you answer without ever touching $p_X$ again: $\mathbb{E}[Y] = 2(2)-3 = 1$ and $\operatorname{var}(Y) = 4(1.6) = 6.4$ come straight from the two numbers of part (d). Part (f) has a quadratic payout $Z = X^2$, and the naive move gives $(\mathbb{E}[X])^2 = 4$ while the truth is $\mathbb{E}[X^2] = 5.6$. The discrepancy is not noise: it is exactly $\operatorname{var}(X) = 1.6$, because $\mathbb{E}[X^2] - (\mathbb{E}[X])^2$ is the variance. So part (f) is a disguised measurement of the spread of the marksman's hit count — a convex payout pays for variability — and it is the reason the identity $\mathbb{E}[X^2] = \operatorname{var}(X)+(\mathbb{E}[X])^2$ is worth memorizing in both directions: read left-to-right it computes a second moment, read right-to-left it computes a variance, which is how §3 will use it throughout.
    Practice 2.7. The same marksman now takes $20$ shots (still $p=0.2$, independent). Find $\mathbb{E}[X]$, $\operatorname{var}(X)$, $\mathbb{E}[X^2]$, and $\mathbf{P}(\text{no hits})$. Did the probability of an empty scorecard go up or down, and by what factor?
    Solution

    With $n=20$, $p=0.2$: $\mathbb{E}[X] = np = 20(0.2) = 4$ and $\operatorname{var}(X) = np(1-p) = 20(0.2)(0.8) = 3.2$ (both confirmed by direct summation over the $21$ masses, keys pq_mk20_EX, pq_mk20_var). Then $$\mathbb{E}[X^2] = \operatorname{var}(X) + (\mathbb{E}[X])^2 = 3.2 + 16 = 19.2 .$$ $$\mathbf{P}(\text{no hits}) = (0.8)^{20} = 0.01152922 .$$ Compared with $(0.8)^{10} = 0.10737$ this is smaller by the factor $(0.8)^{10} = 0.1074$ — i.e. about $9.3$ times less likely, since $\mathbf{P}(\text{no hits in }20) = \big(\mathbf{P}(\text{no hits in }10)\big)^2$ by independence.

    Practice 2.8. Redesign the payout in rec05 P2(e): the range now charges an entry fee $f$ and pays $\$2$ per hit. What fee makes the game fair (expected profit zero)? With that fee, what is the probability the marksman actually loses money?
    Solution

    Profit is $Y = 2X - f$, a linear function, so $\mathbb{E}[Y] = 2\mathbb{E}[X] - f = 2(2) - f = 4 - f$. Fairness means $\mathbb{E}[Y]=0$, i.e. $f = \$4$ — the expected number of hits times the price per hit.

    He loses money when $2X - 4 < 0$, i.e. $X < 2$, i.e. $X \in \{0,1\}$: $$\mathbf{P}(X \le 1) = p_X(0)+p_X(1) = 0.107374 + 0.268436 = 0.375810 .$$ He breaks exactly even when $X=2$ (probability $0.301990$) and profits only when $X\ge 3$ (probability $1 - 0.375810 - 0.301990 = 0.322200$). So a "fair" game is one he loses more often than he wins — the mean is balanced by the big payouts in the thin right tail, exactly the torque picture of Fig. 2.1.

    2.5 The bus paradox: size-biased versus uniform sampling (rec05 P3)

    Example 2.5 (rec05 P3). Four buses carrying $148$ job-seeking MIT students arrive at a job convention. The buses carry $40$, $33$, $25$, and $50$ students, respectively. One of the students is randomly selected; let $X$ denote the number of students that were on the bus carrying this randomly selected student. One of the $4$ bus drivers is also randomly selected; let $Y$ denote the number of students on his bus.
    (a) Which of $\mathbb{E}[X]$ or $\mathbb{E}[Y]$ do you think is larger? Give your reasoning in words.
    (b) Compute $\mathbb{E}[X]$ and $\mathbb{E}[Y]$.
    Solution

    (a) The verbal answer. $\mathbb{E}[X]$ is larger. The two experiments sample the same four buses but through different doors. Picking a driver gives each bus one ticket in the lottery. Picking a student gives each bus as many tickets as it has students — so the $50$-student bus is twice as likely to be selected as the $25$-student bus. Large buses are over-represented when you sample through the passengers, and since "large bus" is exactly the quantity being averaged, the average comes out high. The official solution puts it in one line: "we expect $\mathbb{E}[X]$ to be higher than $\mathbb{E}[Y]$ since if we choose the student, we are more likely to pick a bus with more students."

    (b) Step 1 — the PMF of $Y$ (driver sampled). There are $4$ drivers, one per bus, chosen uniformly, so each bus is picked with probability $1/4$: $$p_Y(y) = \begin{cases} 1/4, & y = 40,\,33,\,25,\,50,\\ 0, & \text{otherwise.}\end{cases}$$ Then $$\mathbb{E}[Y] = 40\cdot\tfrac14 + 33\cdot\tfrac14 + 25\cdot\tfrac14 + 50\cdot\tfrac14 = \frac{40+33+25+50}{4} = \frac{148}{4} = \boxed{37}.$$ This is the plain average of the four bus sizes — total students divided by number of buses.

    Step 2 — the PMF of $X$ (student sampled). The official solution states this PMF without justifying it; here is the justification. The experiment is: pick one of the $148$ students uniformly at random, so each student has probability $1/148$. The event $\{X = x\}$ — "the chosen student's bus carried $x$ students" — consists of exactly those students who rode a bus of size $x$; there are $x$ of them (all four sizes here are distinct, so exactly one bus has size $x$). By the discrete uniform law, probability = (number of favorable outcomes)/(total outcomes): $$p_X(x) = \frac{\#\{\text{students on the bus of size } x\}}{148} = \frac{x}{148}, \qquad x \in \{40,33,25,50\}.$$ Explicitly, $$p_X(40) = \tfrac{40}{148} = 0.2703,\quad p_X(33) = \tfrac{33}{148}=0.2230,\quad p_X(25)=\tfrac{25}{148}=0.1689,\quad p_X(50)=\tfrac{50}{148}=0.3378,$$ and these sum to $\frac{40+33+25+50}{148} = \frac{148}{148} = 1$ ✓ (normalization is automatic because every student is on exactly one bus). A PMF proportional to the value itself is called size-biased.

    Step 3 — $\mathbb{E}[X]$, with the arithmetic the solution omits. $$\begin{aligned} \mathbb{E}[X] &= 40\cdot\frac{40}{148} + 33\cdot\frac{33}{148} + 25\cdot\frac{25}{148} + 50\cdot\frac{50}{148}\\ &= \frac{40^2 + 33^2 + 25^2 + 50^2}{148} = \frac{1600 + 1089 + 625 + 2500}{148} = \frac{5814}{148} = \frac{2907}{74} = 39.283784 \approx \boxed{39.28}. \end{aligned}$$ (Key bus_EX; the recitation prints $39.28$.) So indeed $$\mathbb{E}[X] = 39.28 \;>\; 37 = \mathbb{E}[Y], \qquad \text{gap } = \frac{169}{74} = 2.283784 .$$ Note the structural fact hiding in the algebra: $\mathbb{E}[X] = \frac{\sum_i n_i^2}{\sum_i n_i}$ — a ratio of the second moment to the first — while $\mathbb{E}[Y] = \frac{\sum_i n_i}{4}$ is the plain mean, where $n_i$ denotes the size of bus $i$.

    Fig. 2.4 — Size-biased versus uniform sampling (rec05 P3). Same four buses, two sampling schemes. Sampling a driver gives the uniform PMF $p_Y \equiv 1/4$ with mean $37$; sampling a student gives $p_X(x) = x/148$, which tilts mass toward the big buses and pushes the mean up to $39.28$. Numbers from computes/g2_s2.py keys bus_*.
    Interpretation — why the sampled-bus mean must exceed the driver-sampled mean. The gap is not an accident of these four numbers; it is an identity. Write $n_1,\dots,n_4$ for the bus sizes and let $Y$ be a uniformly chosen one of them. Then $$\mathbb{E}[X] = \frac{\sum_i n_i^2}{\sum_i n_i} = \frac{\frac14\sum_i n_i^2}{\frac14\sum_i n_i} = \frac{\mathbb{E}[Y^2]}{\mathbb{E}[Y]} = \frac{\operatorname{var}(Y) + (\mathbb{E}[Y])^2}{\mathbb{E}[Y]} = \mathbb{E}[Y] + \frac{\operatorname{var}(Y)}{\mathbb{E}[Y]},$$ using the identity $\mathbb{E}[Y^2] = \operatorname{var}(Y) + (\mathbb{E}[Y])^2$ from Example 2.3(c). Numerically, $\mathbb{E}[Y^2] = \frac{5814}{4} = 1453.5$, so $\operatorname{var}(Y) = 1453.5 - 37^2 = 1453.5 - 1369 = 84.5$, and $$\mathbb{E}[Y] + \frac{\operatorname{var}(Y)}{\mathbb{E}[Y]} = 37 + \frac{84.5}{37} = 37 + 2.283784 = 39.283784 = \mathbb{E}[X]. \ \checkmark$$ Since $\operatorname{var}(Y)\ge 0$ and $\mathbb{E}[Y] > 0$, we get $\mathbb{E}[X] \ge \mathbb{E}[Y]$ always, with equality if and only if all buses are the same size ($\operatorname{var}(Y)=0$; check: four buses of $37$ give $\mathbb{E}[X] = \frac{4\cdot 37^2}{148} = 37$, key bus_equal_EX). The excess is exactly the variance-to-mean ratio: the more unequal the bus loads, the worse the bias.
    Gotcha — this is everywhere, and it is usually invisible. The same arithmetic explains a long list of "paradoxes", all of which are the same computation:
    • Class sizes. A university advertises an average class size of $37$ (that is $\mathbb{E}[Y]$, averaging over classes). Survey students and the average class they sit in is $39.28$ — nobody lied.
    • Waiting for a bus. Arrive at random and you are more likely to land inside a long gap between buses than a short one, so your average wait exceeds half the average headway.
    • "Your friends have more friends than you do." Sampling a person through a friendship link is sampling proportional to degree.
    The error to avoid: quoting a mean without saying what was sampled uniformly. Both $37$ and $39.28$ are correct means of the same four numbers $\{40,33,25,50\}$; they answer different questions. Note also that adding an empty bus changes $\mathbb{E}[Y]$ (to $148/5 = 29.6$) but leaves $\mathbb{E}[X]$ at $39.28$ — no student is ever sampled from it (key bus_alt_EX).
    Practice 2.9. Three buses carry $10$, $20$, and $60$ students. Compute $\mathbb{E}[Y]$ (driver sampled) and $\mathbb{E}[X]$ (student sampled), and verify the identity $\mathbb{E}[X] = \mathbb{E}[Y] + \operatorname{var}(Y)/\mathbb{E}[Y]$.
    Solution

    Total students $= 10+20+60 = 90$; three buses. $$\mathbb{E}[Y] = \frac{10+20+60}{3} = \frac{90}{3} = 30 .$$ $$\mathbb{E}[X] = \frac{10^2+20^2+60^2}{90} = \frac{100+400+3600}{90} = \frac{4100}{90} = \frac{410}{9} = 45.5556 .$$ Check the identity: $\mathbb{E}[Y^2] = \frac{4100}{3} = 1366.667$, so $\operatorname{var}(Y) = 1366.667 - 30^2 = 466.667$, and $$\mathbb{E}[Y] + \frac{\operatorname{var}(Y)}{\mathbb{E}[Y]} = 30 + \frac{466.667}{30} = 30 + 15.5556 = 45.5556 . \ \checkmark$$ (Keys pq_bus_EY, pq_bus_EX.) The bias is far worse here ($+51.9\%$) than in rec05 P3 ($+6.2\%$) because the loads are far more unequal.

    Practice 2.10. Suppose all you are told is that $\mathbb{E}[Y] = 37$ and $\mathbb{E}[X] = 39.28$ for some collection of buses. What can you deduce about the spread of the bus sizes?
    Solution

    Rearranging the identity, $\operatorname{var}(Y) = \mathbb{E}[Y]\big(\mathbb{E}[X]-\mathbb{E}[Y]\big) = 37 \times 2.2838 = 84.5$ (the gap must be carried unrounded: $\mathbb{E}[X]-\mathbb{E}[Y] = \frac{169}{74} = 2.283784$, and $37\times\frac{169}{74} = \frac{169}{2} = 84.5$; the rounded $37\times 2.28 = 84.36$ is off by $0.14$), so the standard deviation of the bus sizes is $\sqrt{84.5} = 9.19$ students. The two means determine the variance of the sizes — the excess of the size-biased mean over the plain mean is a direct readout of dispersion. (Cross-check with the actual sizes $\{40,33,25,50\}$: their variance is $84.5$, key bus_varY. ✓)

    2.6 The St. Petersburg paradox: infinite expectation (rec05 P4)

    Example 2.6 (rec05 P4 = B&T Problem 2.21, p. 123). You toss independently a fair coin and count the number of tosses until the first tail appears. If this number is $n$, you receive $2^n$ dollars. What is the expected amount that you will receive? How much would you be willing to pay to play this game?
    Solution

    Step 1 — the PMF of the toss count. (The official solution writes the sum down directly; this is the missing derivation.) Let $N$ be the toss on which the first tail appears. The event $\{N = k\}$ is one specific sequence: heads on tosses $1,\dots,k-1$, then a tail on toss $k$. The tosses are independent and fair, so multiply the per-toss probabilities: $$\mathbf{P}(N = k) = \underbrace{\tfrac12 \cdot \tfrac12 \cdots \tfrac12}_{k-1 \text{ heads}} \cdot \underbrace{\tfrac12}_{\text{one tail}} = \Big(\frac12\Big)^{k-1}\cdot\frac12 = \Big(\frac12\Big)^{k} = 2^{-k}, \qquad k = 1,2,\dots$$ This is the geometric PMF of L05 slide 3 with $p = 1/2$ (with "success" $=$ tail). Sanity check on normalization, using the geometric series $\sum_{k\ge1} r^k = \frac{r}{1-r}$ with $r=\frac12$: $$\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} 2^{-k} = \frac{1/2}{1 - 1/2} = 1 . \ \checkmark$$ The payout is the random variable $W = 2^{N}$, so $W$ takes the value $2^k$ with probability $2^{-k}$: $\$2$ with probability $\tfrac12$, $\$4$ with probability $\tfrac14$, $\$8$ with probability $\tfrac18$, and so on.

    Step 2 — the expectation. By the expected value rule with $g(n) = 2^n$ (§2.2), or equivalently by the definition applied to $W$: $$\mathbb{E}[W] = \sum_{k=1}^{\infty} 2^k\,\mathbf{P}(N=k) = \sum_{k=1}^{\infty} 2^k \cdot 2^{-k} = \sum_{k=1}^{\infty} 1 = 1 + 1 + 1 + \cdots = \boxed{\infty}.$$ Every single term equals exactly $\$1$: doubling the prize precisely offsets halving the probability, so no term ever decays and the series diverges. The partial sums are $\sum_{k=1}^{m} 1 = m$ — after accounting for the first $m$ possible stopping tosses you have accumulated exactly $\$m$ of expected payout, and this grows without bound (keys sp_partial_m*).

    Step 3 — how much would you pay? The official solution's answer: "if you are faced with the choice of playing for a given fee $f$ or not playing at all, and your objective is to maximize your expected net gain, you would be willing to pay any value of $f$" — because $\mathbb{E}[W - f] = \infty - f = \infty$ for every finite $f$. "However, this is in strong disagreement with the behavior of individuals. In fact experiments have shown that most people are willing to pay only about $\$20$ to $\$30$ to play the game. The discrepancy is due to a presumption that the amount one is willing to pay is determined by the expected gain. However, expected gain does not take into account a person's attitude towards risk taking."

    Step 4 — what the infinity actually means (an honest reading). Three separate observations, all computable:

    • Typical payouts are tiny. $\mathbf{P}(W = 2) = \tfrac12$, $\mathbf{P}(W \le 4) = \tfrac34 = 0.75$, $\mathbf{P}(W\le 8) = \tfrac78 = 0.875$, $\mathbf{P}(W \le 16) = \tfrac{15}{16} = 0.9375$. The median payout is $\$2$. Paying the "reasonable" $\$20$ fee, you lose money on $93.75\%$ of plays (keys sp_P_payout_le_2pow*).
    • The infinity lives entirely in a vanishing tail. $\mathbf{P}(W \ge 1024) = \mathbf{P}(N \ge 10) = 2^{-9} = 0.00195$. Those rare plays carry all the expectation — the torque picture of Fig. 2.1 taken to its limit, with mass going to zero exactly as fast as distance goes to infinity.
    • Sample averages do not settle down. The recitation's Extra Handout shows histograms of simulated play: "$20$ simulations, observed average $=\$19.20$" and "$200$ simulations, observed average $=\$11.16$" — the larger experiment produced the smaller average, which cannot happen systematically for a variable with a finite mean. Our own simulation (seed $6041$, key sp_sim_*) gives averages $\$7.00$ ($20$ plays), $\$11.98$ ($200$), $\$274.95$ ($2000$), $\$32.60$ ($20{,}000$), $\$16.25$ ($200{,}000$): no convergence, just occasional enormous jumps whenever a long head-run occurs. The law of large numbers does not apply, because it requires a finite mean.
    • The bank is not infinite. No counterparty can pay $2^{100}$ dollars. Cap the payout and the expectation becomes small and finite — see the derivation below and the widget.

    Step 5 — the truncated game (the fix, derived). Suppose the house can afford at most $m$ doublings, i.e. it pays $\min(2^N, 2^m)$: you get $2^N$ if the first tail comes on toss $N \le m$, and the cap $2^m$ if the coin produces $m$ heads in a row. Split the expectation over the two cases: $$\mathbb{E}\big[2^{\min(N,m)}\big] = \underbrace{\sum_{k=1}^{m} 2^k\,2^{-k}}_{\text{game ends by toss } m} + \underbrace{2^m\,\mathbf{P}(N > m)}_{\text{cap paid}} .$$ The first sum has $m$ terms each equal to $1$, so it equals $m$. For the second, $\{N > m\}$ means the first $m$ tosses were all heads, which has probability $(1/2)^m = 2^{-m}$, so the term is $2^m \cdot 2^{-m} = 1$. Therefore $$\boxed{\ \mathbb{E}\big[\text{capped payout}\big] = m + 1\ }$$ — exactly one dollar per additional doubling the house can cover. (If instead the house simply forfeits and pays $\$0$ when the run exceeds $m$, the same split gives $m + 0 = m$.) Numbers: a house able to pay $2^{10} = \$1{,}024$ has a game worth $\$11$; $2^{20} = \$1{,}048{,}576$ makes it worth $\$21$; $2^{30} \approx \$1.07$ billion makes it worth $\$31$ (keys sp_cap_m10, sp_cap_m20, sp_cap_m30). The experimentally observed willingness to pay of $\$20$–$\$30$ corresponds to believing your counterparty is good for somewhere between a million and a billion dollars — which is a perfectly rational belief.

    Fig. 2.5 — Why the St. Petersburg expectation diverges, and what that looks like in data (rec05 P4 and Extra Handout). Left: each possible stopping toss $k$ contributes $2^k \cdot 2^{-k} = \$1$ to the expectation, so the partial sums grow linearly and never converge. Right: running average payout for four independent simulated sequences of $20{,}000$ plays (seed $6041$, log–log axes). Each vertical jump is a single lucky play; between jumps the average decays like $1/n$. Compare the handout's two runs, which averaged $\$19.20$ over $20$ plays and $\$11.16$ over $200$.
    Gotcha — infinite expectation does not mean large typical winnings. The mean is a torque balance, not a forecast. Here the balance point runs off to infinity because mass $2^{-k}$ sits at distance $2^k$, yet the outcome you will actually see is small: $\$2$ half the time, at most $\$16$ with probability $0.9375$. Three practical corollaries:
    1. Never quote a mean without a spread (or a quantile). "Expected payout $=\infty$" and "median payout $=\$2$" describe the same game.
    2. Check that the mean exists before using it. B&T §2.4 (footnote p. 82) requires $\sum_x |x|p_X(x) < \infty$, and offers this exact variable — value $2^k$ with probability $2^{-k}$ — as its counterexample. Averages of samples from such a variable do not converge (Fig. 2.5, right).
    3. Maximizing expected money is not the same as rational choice. The recitation's own conclusion: "expected gain does not take into account a person's attitude towards risk taking." Replace dollars by utility (e.g. $\log$ of wealth) and the expectation becomes finite; or, more prosaically, cap the bankroll and the game is worth $m+1$ dollars.
    Practice 2.11. Same game, but the coin is biased with $\mathbf{P}(\text{tail}) = 3/4$ on each toss (so the run of heads ends sooner). Is the expected payout still infinite? Compute it.
    Solution

    Now $\mathbf{P}(N=k) = (1/4)^{k-1}(3/4)$ — $k-1$ heads, each of probability $1/4$, then a tail of probability $3/4$. The payout is still $2^k$, so $$\mathbb{E}[W] = \sum_{k=1}^{\infty} 2^k \Big(\frac14\Big)^{k-1}\frac34 = \frac34\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} 2^k \Big(\frac14\Big)^{k-1} = \frac34 \cdot 2 \sum_{j=0}^{\infty} \Big(\frac{2}{4}\Big)^{j} = \frac32 \cdot \frac{1}{1 - \frac12} = \frac32 \cdot 2 = 3 ,$$ substituting $j = k-1$ so that $2^k(1/4)^{k-1} = 2\cdot(2/4)^{j} = 2(1/2)^j$, and summing the geometric series with ratio $1/2 < 1$. So the expectation is finite: $\$3$ (verified numerically, key pq_sp_variant_E $=3.000000$). The divergence in the original game was a knife's edge: it needs the payout to double exactly as fast as the probability halves. Any faster decay of the probability (ratio $2(1-q) < 1$, i.e. $q > 1/2$) makes the series converge.

    Practice 2.12. A casino offers the fair-coin St. Petersburg game but can pay at most $\$1{,}048{,}576 = 2^{20}$. It charges $\$25$ to play. Is the game favorable to the player? What is the probability the cap is actually reached?
    Solution

    With $m = 20$ the capped expectation is $\mathbb{E}[\text{payout}] = m+1 = \$21$ (Step 5). The expected net gain is $21 - 25 = -\$4$ per play, so the game is unfavorable — despite the uncapped version having infinite expectation. The cap is reached when the first $20$ tosses are all heads, with probability $2^{-20} = 9.537\times 10^{-7}$, about one play in a million (keys sp_cap_m20, sp_P_hit_cap_m20). Note how little it takes to flip the verdict: at a $\$20$ fee the game would be favorable by $\$1$ per play, and the player would still lose on $93.75\%$ of plays.

    §2 cheatsheet.
    • Definition (L05 slide 6): $\mathbb{E}[X] = \sum_x x\,p_X(x)$ — a weighted average, and the balance point of the PMF: $\sum_x (x - \mathbb{E}[X])p_X(x) = 0$. Well-defined only when $\sum_x|x|p_X(x) < \infty$ (B&T §2.4 footnote p. 82).
    • Uniform on $\{0,\dots,n\}$: $\mathbb{E}[X] = \frac{1}{n+1}\cdot\frac{n(n+1)}{2} = n/2$ (slide 6 blank, filled).
    • Expected value rule (slide 7, rec05 P1a): $\mathbb{E}[g(X)] = \sum_x g(x)p_X(x)$ — never build $p_Y$ just to get a mean.
    • Linearity (slide 7 blanks): $\mathbb{E}[\alpha]=\alpha$; $\mathbb{E}[\alpha X] = \alpha\mathbb{E}[X]$; $\mathbb{E}[\alpha X+\beta] = \alpha\mathbb{E}[X]+\beta$. And $\operatorname{var}(\alpha X+\beta) = \alpha^2\operatorname{var}(X)$.
    • Caution: $\mathbb{E}[g(X)] \ne g(\mathbb{E}[X])$ unless $g$ is linear (Alice's speed vs. time: $16$ min correct, $8$ min wrong).
    • Second moment: $\mathbb{E}[X^2] = \sum_x x^2p_X(x) = \operatorname{var}(X) + (\mathbb{E}[X])^2 \ \ge (\mathbb{E}[X])^2$.
    • Marksman (rec05 P2), $X\sim\text{Bin}(10,0.2)$: $p_X(0) = 0.8^{10} = 0.1074$; $\mathbf{P}(X\ge6) = 0.006369$; $\mathbb{E}[X]=2$, $\operatorname{var}(X)=1.6$; $Y=2X-3 \Rightarrow \mathbb{E}[Y]=1$, $\operatorname{var}(Y)=6.4$; $Z=X^2 \Rightarrow \mathbb{E}[Z]=5.6$.
    • Size bias (rec05 P3): sample a student and $p_X(x) = x/148$, giving $\mathbb{E}[X] = 5814/148 = 39.28$; sample a driver and $p_Y \equiv 1/4$, giving $\mathbb{E}[Y]=37$. Always $\mathbb{E}[X] = \mathbb{E}[Y] + \operatorname{var}(Y)/\mathbb{E}[Y] \ge \mathbb{E}[Y]$.
    • St. Petersburg (rec05 P4): $\mathbf{P}(N=k)=2^{-k}$, payout $2^k$, every term contributes $\$1$, so $\mathbb{E}[W]=\infty$ — while the median payout is $\$2$. Cap the house at $2^m$ and the game is worth exactly $m+1$ dollars.

    §3   Variance, conditioning, and the geometric distribution

    Sources: L06 slides 1–7 (raster p01–p02) · rec06 P3 (= B&T Example 2.17, pp. 105–106) · rec07 P3 (= B&T Problem 2.33, pp. 128–129) · B&T §2.2 (geometric PMF), B&T §2.4 (mean and variance), B&T §2.6 (conditioning, total expectation, B&T Example 2.17)

    Section 2 built the PMF and the expectation. Expectation is one number, and one number cannot describe a random variable: the constant $5$ and a coin-flip that pays $0$ or $10$ have the same mean and nothing else in common. This section adds the second number — the variance — and then adds the single most productive technique in the whole course: conditioning a random variable on an event, followed by averaging the conditional answers back together (the total expectation theorem). Those two ideas turn otherwise brutal infinite sums into one-line algebra, which is exactly how the geometric distribution's mean $1/p$ and variance $(1-p)/p^2$ fall out, and how "how long until two heads in a row?" becomes a $2\times2$ linear system.

    The section is organized as: the centered variable and why it is useless by itself (§3.1); variance and its two computational rules (§3.2); the trap $\mathbb{E}[g(X)] \neq g(\mathbb{E}[X])$, worked on the lecture's random-speed example (§3.3); conditional PMFs and conditional expectations (§3.4); the geometric PMF and its memorylessness (§3.5, with an interactive check); the total expectation theorem (§3.6); the mean and variance of the geometric (§3.7); and state recursions for waiting-time problems (§3.8).

    Fig. 3.1 — Decision guide for the whole section: which tool computes $\mathbb{E}[X]$ (L06 slides 2–7). Each leaf is derived below; the "restart" branch is §3.6–§3.8.

    3.1 The centered variable, and why $\mathbb{E}\big[X - \mathbb{E}[X]\big] = 0$

    L06 slide 2 reviews the machinery of Section 2 and then poses one question with the answer left blank. Here $X$ is a discrete random variable with PMF $p_X$, and we write $\mu = \mathbb{E}[X]$ for its mean — a single fixed number, not a random variable.

    Example 3.1 (L06 slide 2 — the blank on the slide). Evaluate $\mathbb{E}\big[X - \mathbb{E}[X]\big]$.

    Solution — route 1, using the linearity rule already on the slide. The slide states $\mathbb{E}[\alpha X + \beta] = \alpha\,\mathbb{E}[X] + \beta$ for constants $\alpha,\beta$. The quantity $X - \mathbb{E}[X]$ is of exactly this form with $\alpha = 1$ and $\beta = -\mu$ (legal, because $\mu$ is a constant once the PMF is fixed). Therefore $$\mathbb{E}\big[X - \mathbb{E}[X]\big] \;=\; \mathbb{E}[1\cdot X + (-\mu)] \;=\; 1\cdot\mathbb{E}[X] + (-\mu) \;=\; \mu - \mu \;=\; 0.$$

    Solution — route 2, from the expected-value rule, so nothing is assumed. Apply $\mathbb{E}[g(X)] = \sum_x g(x)p_X(x)$ with $g(x) = x - \mu$: $$\mathbb{E}\big[X - \mu\big] \;=\; \sum_x (x - \mu)\,p_X(x) \;=\; \sum_x x\,p_X(x) \;-\; \sum_x \mu\, p_X(x),$$ splitting the sum (a finite/absolutely convergent sum may be split term by term). The first sum is $\mathbb{E}[X] = \mu$ by definition. In the second, $\mu$ does not depend on the summation index $x$, so it factors out: $$\sum_x \mu\,p_X(x) \;=\; \mu \sum_x p_X(x) \;=\; \mu \cdot 1 \;=\; \mu,$$ using the normalization property $\sum_x p_X(x) = 1$ of any PMF. Hence $\mathbb{E}[X-\mu] = \mu - \mu = 0$. $\blacksquare$

    Numerical sanity check (computes/g2_s3.py, keys A_EX, A_E_centered). Take a deliberately lopsided PMF: $p_X(-3)=1/8$, $p_X(0)=3/8$, $p_X(2)=1/4$, $p_X(7)=1/4$ (these sum to $1$). Then $$\mu = (-3)\tfrac18 + 0\cdot\tfrac38 + 2\cdot\tfrac14 + 7\cdot\tfrac14 = -\tfrac{3}{8} + 0 + \tfrac{1}{2} + \tfrac{7}{4} = \tfrac{15}{8} = 1.875,$$ and $$\mathbb{E}[X-\mu] = \left(-3-\tfrac{15}{8}\right)\tfrac18 + \left(0-\tfrac{15}{8}\right)\tfrac38 + \left(2-\tfrac{15}{8}\right)\tfrac14 + \left(7-\tfrac{15}{8}\right)\tfrac14 = -\tfrac{39}{64} - \tfrac{45}{64} + \tfrac{1}{32} + \tfrac{41}{32} = -\tfrac{84}{64} + \tfrac{84}{64} = 0,$$ exactly $0$, as the algebra promised.

    Interpretation. $X - \mathbb{E}[X]$ is called the centered or deviation variable: it measures how far above or below its own mean $X$ landed. The result says the positive deviations and the negative deviations exactly balance, weighted by probability. In physical terms, $\mu$ is the center of mass of the probability distribution, and the total torque about the center of mass is zero. That is the good news; the bad news is that it makes $\mathbb{E}[X-\mu]$ completely useless as a measure of spread, because it is $0$ for every random variable — the wildest and the tamest alike. To measure spread you must destroy the cancellation, and the standard way is to square the deviation before averaging. That is §3.2.
    Gotcha — $\mathbb{E}[X]$ is a number, $X$ is a function. The expression $X - \mathbb{E}[X]$ mixes a random variable with a constant, which is fine. But $\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[X]\big] = \mathbb{E}[X]$ (the expectation of a constant is that constant), and $\mathbb{E}[X]$ never carries a subscript $x$ — writing $\sum_x \mathbb{E}[X] p_X(x)$ and "simplifying" it to $\mathbb{E}[X]\sum_x p_X(x)$ is legal precisely because $\mathbb{E}[X]$ has no $x$ in it. Confusing the two is the single most common early error.
    Practice 3.1. Let $X$ have PMF $p_X(1) = 0.2$, $p_X(4) = 0.5$, $p_X(9) = 0.3$. (a) Verify by direct summation that $\mathbb{E}[X - \mathbb{E}[X]] = 0$. (b) Compute $\mathbb{E}\big[\,|X - \mathbb{E}[X]|\,\big]$ and explain why it is not zero.
    Solution

    (a) $\mathbb{E}[X] = 1(0.2) + 4(0.5) + 9(0.3) = 0.2 + 2.0 + 2.7 = 4.9$. The deviations are $1 - 4.9 = -3.9$, $4-4.9 = -0.9$, $9 - 4.9 = 4.1$, so $$\mathbb{E}[X-4.9] = (-3.9)(0.2) + (-0.9)(0.5) + (4.1)(0.3) = -0.78 - 0.45 + 1.23 = 0.$$

    (b) $\mathbb{E}[|X-4.9|] = 3.9(0.2) + 0.9(0.5) + 4.1(0.3) = 0.78 + 0.45 + 1.23 = 2.46$. Taking the absolute value first removes the signs, so nothing cancels; only after the signs are gone does the average distance become an honest measure of spread. (This mean absolute deviation is a legitimate spread measure — it is just algebraically far clumsier than the squared version, because $|\cdot|$ has no expansion rule while $(\cdot)^2$ does.)

    3.2 Variance and standard deviation

    Definition (variance, standard deviation — L06 slide 2; B&T §2.4). For a discrete random variable $X$ with mean $\mu = \mathbb{E}[X]$, $$\operatorname{var}(X) \;=\; \mathbb{E}\big[(X - \mathbb{E}[X])^2\big] \;=\; \sum_x (x-\mu)^2\, p_X(x),$$ the probability-weighted average of the squared deviation from the mean. Its square root $$\sigma_X \;=\; \sqrt{\operatorname{var}(X)}$$ is the standard deviation. Here $x$ ranges over the values $X$ can take and $p_X(x)$ is the probability of each.

    Two facts make the variance workable. The first says how it reacts to a linear change of units; the second is the formula you will actually compute with.

    Property 1 (L06 slide 2). For constants $a$ and $b$, $$\operatorname{var}(aX + b) \;=\; a^2 \operatorname{var}(X), \qquad \sigma_{aX+b} = |a|\,\sigma_X.$$
    Derivation — every step

    Put $Y = aX + b$; $Y$ is a new random variable, a rescaled and shifted copy of $X$. Here $a$ is the scale factor and $b$ the shift, both constants.

    Step 1 — its mean. By the linearity rule $\mathbb{E}[\alpha X + \beta] = \alpha\mathbb{E}[X]+\beta$ from L06 slide 2, $$\mathbb{E}[Y] = \mathbb{E}[aX+b] = a\,\mathbb{E}[X] + b = a\mu + b.$$

    Step 2 — its deviation. Subtract: $$Y - \mathbb{E}[Y] \;=\; (aX + b) - (a\mu + b) \;=\; aX - a\mu \;=\; a\,(X - \mu).$$ The shift $b$ cancels exactly — it moved the variable and its mean by the same amount.

    Step 3 — square it. $$\big(Y - \mathbb{E}[Y]\big)^2 = \big(a(X-\mu)\big)^2 = a^2 (X-\mu)^2.$$

    Step 4 — average it. By the expected-value rule with $g(x) = a^2(x-\mu)^2$, $$\operatorname{var}(Y) = \sum_x a^2 (x-\mu)^2 p_X(x) = a^2 \sum_x (x-\mu)^2 p_X(x) = a^2 \operatorname{var}(X),$$ pulling the constant $a^2$ out of the sum in the middle step.

    Step 5 — standard deviation. Take square roots: $\sigma_Y = \sqrt{a^2\operatorname{var}(X)} = \sqrt{a^2}\,\sqrt{\operatorname{var}(X)} = |a|\,\sigma_X$. The absolute value is essential — $\sqrt{a^2} = |a|$, not $a$, and a standard deviation can never be negative. $\blacksquare$

    Numerical check (computes/g2_s3.py, keys G_varY, G_a2_varX). Let $X$ be uniform on $\{1,2,3,4\}$, so $\mathbb{E}[X] = 5/2$ and (from Property 2 below) $\operatorname{var}(X) = 15/2 - 25/4 = 5/4$. Take $a = -3$, $b = 7$: then $Y$ takes the values $4, 1, -2, -5$ each with probability $1/4$, so $\mathbb{E}[Y] = (4+1-2-5)/4 = -1/2$ (which is indeed $-3(5/2)+7$), $\mathbb{E}[Y^2] = (16+1+4+25)/4 = 46/4 = 23/2$, and $$\operatorname{var}(Y) = \tfrac{23}{2} - \left(-\tfrac12\right)^2 = \tfrac{46}{4} - \tfrac14 = \tfrac{45}{4} = 11.25 \;=\; (-3)^2 \cdot \tfrac54 = 9 \cdot 1.25. \checkmark$$

    Property 2 (the computational formula — L06 slide 2). $$\operatorname{var}(X) \;=\; \mathbb{E}[X^2] \;-\; \big(\mathbb{E}[X]\big)^2.$$
    Derivation — the algebra L06 slide 2 asserts but does not show

    Write $\mu = \mathbb{E}[X]$, a constant. Start from the definition and expand the square inside the sum: $$\operatorname{var}(X) \;=\; \sum_x (x - \mu)^2 p_X(x) \;=\; \sum_x \big(x^2 - 2\mu x + \mu^2\big) p_X(x),$$ using only the binomial expansion $(x-\mu)^2 = x^2 - 2\mu x + \mu^2$.

    Split into three sums (legal term by term): $$= \sum_x x^2 p_X(x) \;-\; \sum_x 2\mu x\, p_X(x) \;+\; \sum_x \mu^2 p_X(x).$$

    Handle them one at a time. Every variable below has a name:

    • $\displaystyle \sum_x x^2 p_X(x) = \mathbb{E}[X^2]$ — this is the expected-value rule with $g(x)=x^2$; $\mathbb{E}[X^2]$ is the second moment.
    • $\displaystyle \sum_x 2\mu x\, p_X(x) = 2\mu \sum_x x\,p_X(x) = 2\mu\cdot\mu = 2\mu^2$ — the constant $2\mu$ comes out of the sum, and what is left is $\mathbb{E}[X] = \mu$.
    • $\displaystyle \sum_x \mu^2 p_X(x) = \mu^2 \sum_x p_X(x) = \mu^2 \cdot 1 = \mu^2$ — again a constant out front, then PMF normalization.

    Assembling: $$\operatorname{var}(X) = \mathbb{E}[X^2] - 2\mu^2 + \mu^2 = \mathbb{E}[X^2] - \mu^2 = \mathbb{E}[X^2] - \big(\mathbb{E}[X]\big)^2. \qquad \blacksquare$$

    Worked instance. $X$ uniform on $\{1,2,3,4\}$ (the PMF on L06 slide 5). Then $$\mathbb{E}[X] = \tfrac{1+2+3+4}{4} = \tfrac{10}{4} = \tfrac52, \qquad \mathbb{E}[X^2] = \tfrac{1+4+9+16}{4} = \tfrac{30}{4} = \tfrac{15}{2},$$ so $\operatorname{var}(X) = \tfrac{15}{2} - \left(\tfrac52\right)^2 = \tfrac{30}{4} - \tfrac{25}{4} = \tfrac{5}{4} = 1.25$ and $\sigma_X = \sqrt{1.25} \approx 1.118034$. (computes/g2_s3.py keys C_EX, C_EX2, C_varX.)

    Interpretation. Read Property 2 as "average of the squares minus square of the average". It is faster than the definition because it needs only two sums over the PMF instead of one sum plus a subtraction inside every term. Read Property 1 as a units statement: variance lives in the square of the units of $X$ (if $X$ is in miles, $\operatorname{var}(X)$ is in square miles), which is why the standard deviation $\sigma_X$ — back in the original units — is the number you quote to a human. Shifting a variable by $b$ moves the whole picture without changing its width, so $b$ vanishes; stretching by $a$ stretches every deviation by $a$, and squared deviations by $a^2$.
    Gotcha — $\operatorname{var}(aX+b) \neq a\operatorname{var}(X)+b$. Variance is not linear. The two errors to never make: keeping the $+b$ (it always dies), and forgetting to square the $a$. A quick self-check: $\operatorname{var}(-X) = (-1)^2\operatorname{var}(X) = \operatorname{var}(X)$ — flipping a variable cannot change its spread, and any "rule" that gives $-\operatorname{var}(X)$ is broken, since variance is a sum of squares times probabilities and hence never negative.
    Gotcha — $\mathbb{E}[X^2] \ge (\mathbb{E}[X])^2$, always. Property 2 plus $\operatorname{var}(X)\ge 0$ forces this, with equality only when $\operatorname{var}(X)=0$, i.e. when $X$ is a constant with probability 1. So if a calculation ever produces $\mathbb{E}[X^2] < (\mathbb{E}[X])^2$, one of the two moments is wrong. This is a free correctness check on every variance you compute — use it.
    Practice 3.2. $X$ is uniform on $\{0,1,2,3,4,5\}$. Find $\mathbb{E}[X]$, $\operatorname{var}(X)$, $\sigma_X$, and then $\operatorname{var}(10 - 4X)$.
    Solution

    Each value has probability $1/6$. $\mathbb{E}[X] = \frac{0+1+2+3+4+5}{6} = \frac{15}{6} = \frac52 = 2.5$.

    $\mathbb{E}[X^2] = \frac{0+1+4+9+16+25}{6} = \frac{55}{6} \approx 9.166667$.

    $\operatorname{var}(X) = \frac{55}{6} - \left(\frac52\right)^2 = \frac{110}{12} - \frac{75}{12} = \frac{35}{12} \approx 2.916667$. (This matches the general uniform-on-$n$-consecutive-integers formula $(n^2-1)/12 = (36-1)/12 = 35/12$.)

    $\sigma_X = \sqrt{35/12} \approx 1.707825$.

    $\operatorname{var}(10-4X) = (-4)^2\operatorname{var}(X) = 16\cdot\frac{35}{12} = \frac{140}{3} \approx 46.666667$; the $+10$ contributes nothing. (computes/g2_s3.py keys I1_* for $\mathbb{E}[X]$, $\mathbb{E}[X^2]$, $\operatorname{var}(X)$, $\sigma_X$, and I10_var(10-4X) for the last answer.)

    Practice 3.3. $X$ takes the values $-2, 1, 4$ with probabilities $1/4, 1/2, 1/4$. Compute $\mathbb{E}[X]$, $\operatorname{var}(X)$, and then $\mathbb{E}[5-2X]$, $\operatorname{var}(5-2X)$, $\sigma_{5-2X}$.
    Solution

    $\mathbb{E}[X] = (-2)\frac14 + 1\cdot\frac12 + 4\cdot\frac14 = -\frac12 + \frac12 + 1 = 1$.

    $\mathbb{E}[X^2] = 4\cdot\frac14 + 1\cdot\frac12 + 16\cdot\frac14 = 1 + \frac12 + 4 = \frac{11}{2}$.

    $\operatorname{var}(X) = \frac{11}{2} - 1^2 = \frac92 = 4.5$. (Definition check: $\frac14(-3)^2 + \frac12(0)^2 + \frac14(3)^2 = \frac94 + 0 + \frac94 = \frac92$. ✓)

    $\mathbb{E}[5-2X] = 5 - 2(1) = 3$. $\operatorname{var}(5-2X) = (-2)^2 \cdot \frac92 = 18$. $\sigma_{5-2X} = \sqrt{18} = 3\sqrt2 \approx 4.242641$. (computes/g2_s3.py keys I5_*.)

    3.3 The expected-value rule bites: random speed

    L06 slides 3–4 exist to plant one warning: the expectation of a function is not the function of the expectation. The vehicle is a trip of fixed distance $d = 200$ miles traversed at a constant but random speed $V$. The slide's PMF gives $V$ two values: $$p_V(1) = \tfrac12, \qquad p_V(200) = \tfrac12,$$ i.e. the trip is either a crawl at $1$ mph or a rocket at $200$ mph, equally likely. The travel time is the random variable $$T \;=\; t(V) \;=\; \frac{d}{V} \;=\; \frac{200}{V} \quad \text{hours},$$ which the slide gives in red (i.e. as part of the setup). Here $V$ is the speed in miles per hour, $t(\cdot)$ is the deterministic function "distance over speed", and $T$ is the resulting random travel time in hours.

    Example 3.2 (L06 slides 3–4 — the three blanks on slide 3 and the two on slide 4, all solved).

    (i) $\mathbb{E}[V]$. By the definition of expectation, $$\mathbb{E}[V] = \sum_v v\,p_V(v) = 1\cdot\tfrac12 + 200\cdot\tfrac12 = \tfrac12 + 100 = \tfrac{201}{2} = 100.5 \text{ mph}.$$

    (ii) $\operatorname{var}(V)$. First the second moment, via the expected-value rule with $g(v)=v^2$: $$\mathbb{E}[V^2] = 1^2\cdot\tfrac12 + 200^2\cdot\tfrac12 = \tfrac12 + \tfrac{40000}{2} = \tfrac{40001}{2} = 20000.5.$$ Then Property 2 of §3.2: $$\operatorname{var}(V) = \mathbb{E}[V^2] - \big(\mathbb{E}[V]\big)^2 = 20000.5 - (100.5)^2 = 20000.5 - 10100.25 = 9900.25 = \tfrac{39601}{4}.$$ Cross-check from the definition: $\operatorname{var}(V) = \tfrac12(1-100.5)^2 + \tfrac12(200-100.5)^2 = \tfrac12(99.5)^2 + \tfrac12(99.5)^2 = (99.5)^2 = 9900.25$. ✓

    (iii) $\sigma_V$. $\sigma_V = \sqrt{9900.25} = 99.5$ mph. (For any two-point PMF with equal masses $1/2$, the standard deviation is exactly half the gap between the two values: here $(200-1)/2 = 99.5$.)

    (iv) The time in hours, $T = t(V)$. The function $t$ is applied outcome by outcome: $$t(1) = \frac{200}{1} = 200 \text{ hours}, \qquad t(200) = \frac{200}{200} = 1 \text{ hour}.$$ Since $t$ is one-to-one on the two values, $T$ inherits the probabilities: $p_T(200) = 1/2$, $p_T(1) = 1/2$.

    (v) $\mathbb{E}[T]$, by the expected-value rule. The slide writes $\mathbb{E}[T] = \mathbb{E}[t(V)] = \sum_v t(v)p_V(v)$; substituting, $$\mathbb{E}[T] = t(1)\,p_V(1) + t(200)\,p_V(200) = 200\cdot\tfrac12 + 1\cdot\tfrac12 = 100 + \tfrac12 = \tfrac{201}{2} = 100.5 \text{ hours}.$$

    (vi) The comparison the slide is built around. The naive answer "distance over average speed" gives $$\frac{200}{\mathbb{E}[V]} = \frac{200}{100.5} = \frac{400}{201} \approx 1.990050 \text{ hours},$$ whereas the correct expected travel time is $\mathbb{E}[T] = 100.5$ hours. The two differ by a factor of $$\frac{\mathbb{E}[T]}{200/\mathbb{E}[V]} = \frac{100.5}{1.990050} \approx 50.50125,$$ so the naive computation understates the expected trip time by more than a factor of fifty. This is the slide's assertion $\mathbb{E}[200/V] = \mathbb{E}[T] \neq 200/\mathbb{E}[V]$.

    (vii) Filling the slide-4 derivation gap: $\mathbb{E}[T]\,\mathbb{E}[V] \neq 200$. The product $TV$ is identically $200$ — whatever the speed, distance $=$ speed $\times$ time — so $\mathbb{E}[TV] = \mathbb{E}[200] = 200$ with no randomness left. But $$\mathbb{E}[T]\cdot\mathbb{E}[V] = 100.5 \times 100.5 = 10100.25 \;\neq\; 200 = \mathbb{E}[TV].$$ Products of expectations are not expectations of products (that would require independence, and $T$ and $V$ are about as dependent as two variables can be — each determines the other).

    (viii) $\operatorname{var}(T)$ and $\sigma_T$, for completeness. $\mathbb{E}[T^2] = 200^2\cdot\tfrac12 + 1^2\cdot\tfrac12 = \tfrac{40001}{2} = 20000.5$, so $\operatorname{var}(T) = 20000.5 - 100.5^2 = 9900.25$ and $\sigma_T = 99.5$ hours — numerically identical to the speed's, because the PMF of $T$ is the PMF of $V$ with the labels swapped. (All values: computes/g2_s3.py keys B_*.)

    Fig. 3.2 — The random-speed example (L06 slides 3–4). Panels (a) and (b) are the same two-point PMF relabeled; panel (c) is the whole point — plugging the mean speed into $t(v)=200/v$ gives an answer 50 times too small.
    Interpretation — why the gap is so enormous, and which number you actually want. The function $t(v) = 200/v$ is strongly convex: it plunges near $v=0$ and flattens for large $v$. Convexity means a chord lies above the curve, so averaging $t$ at the two endpoints (which is $\mathbb{E}[T]$) lands above the curve's value at the midpoint (which is $t(\mathbb{E}[V]) = 200/\mathbb{E}[V]$). That inequality — $\mathbb{E}[g(X)] \ge g(\mathbb{E}[X])$ for convex $g$ — is Jensen's inequality, and here the gap is $100.5 - 1.990050 \approx 98.51$ hours. Intuitively, the slow trip contributes $200$ hours and dominates the average; the fast trip is over in an hour and contributes almost nothing. The clock spends nearly all its time crawling.

    The practically useful reading: the effective average speed for a fixed-distance trip is not $\mathbb{E}[V]$ but $d/\mathbb{E}[T] = 200/100.5 = 400/201 \approx 1.990050$ mph, which is the harmonic mean $2/(1/1 + 1/200)$ of the two speeds. Harmonic means are what you get when you average rates over a fixed distance; arithmetic means are what you get when you average rates over a fixed time. Choosing the wrong one is the error the slide is warning about.

    Gotcha — $\mathbb{E}[g(X)] = g(\mathbb{E}[X])$ only when $g$ is affine. The rule $\mathbb{E}[\alpha X + \beta] = \alpha\mathbb{E}[X]+\beta$ is true for every $X$ — but $g(x) = \alpha x + \beta$ is the only shape for which "push the expectation inside" is safe. For $g(x)=x^2$: $\mathbb{E}[X^2] \ge (\mathbb{E}[X])^2$, with strict inequality unless $X$ is constant (this is exactly $\operatorname{var}(X)>0$). For $g(x)=1/x$ on $x>0$: $\mathbb{E}[1/X] \ge 1/\mathbb{E}[X]$ whenever $X>0$ — the hypothesis is not optional, since $1/x$ is not convex on any interval containing $0$ (take $X = \pm1$ with probability $1/2$ each: $\mathbb{E}[1/X]=0=\mathbb{E}[X]$ and $1/\mathbb{E}[X]$ is not even defined). Whenever the quantity you want is a nonlinear function of a random variable, compute the PMF of the function and average that.
    Recipe — computing $\mathbb{E}[g(X)]$ safely.
    1. Identify the underlying random variable $X$ and list its values $x$ with $p_X(x)$.
    2. Apply $g$ outcome by outcome: build the table of pairs $\big(g(x),\,p_X(x)\big)$.
    3. If several $x$ map to the same $g(x)$, merge them by adding their probabilities — that is the PMF $p_{g(X)}$.
    4. Sum: $\mathbb{E}[g(X)] = \sum_x g(x)p_X(x)$ (you may sum over the original $x$'s without merging; merging is only needed if you want the PMF itself).
    5. Never shortcut to $g(\mathbb{E}[X])$ unless $g$ is of the form $\alpha x + \beta$.
    6. Sanity-check the direction: for convex $g$ (like $x^2$, $1/x$ on $x>0$, $e^x$) the true answer must be $\ge g(\mathbb{E}[X])$.
    Practice 3.4. A 120-mile drive is made at a constant random speed $V$ that equals 30 mph or 60 mph, each with probability $1/2$. (a) What is $\mathbb{E}[V]$ and what naive travel time does it suggest? (b) What is the true $\mathbb{E}[T]$? (c) What is the effective average speed?
    Solution

    (a) $\mathbb{E}[V] = \frac12(30) + \frac12(60) = 45$ mph, suggesting $120/45 = 8/3 \approx 2.666667$ hours.

    (b) $T = 120/V$ takes the values $120/30 = 4$ h and $120/60 = 2$ h, each with probability $1/2$, so $$\mathbb{E}[T] = \tfrac12(4) + \tfrac12(2) = 3 \text{ hours}.$$ The naive figure is 20 minutes too optimistic.

    (c) Effective speed $= 120/\mathbb{E}[T] = 120/3 = 40$ mph — the harmonic mean $2/(1/30 + 1/60) = 2/(3/60) = 40$, which is less than the arithmetic mean 45, as convexity requires. (computes/g2_s3.py keys I2_*.)

    Practice 3.5. Let $X$ be the number of heads in two independent fair coin tosses, so $p_X(0)=1/4$, $p_X(1)=1/2$, $p_X(2)=1/4$. Compare $\mathbb{E}[X^2]$ with $(\mathbb{E}[X])^2$ and say what the difference equals.
    Solution

    $\mathbb{E}[X] = 0(\frac14) + 1(\frac12) + 2(\frac14) = 1$, so $(\mathbb{E}[X])^2 = 1$.

    $\mathbb{E}[X^2] = 0(\frac14) + 1(\frac12) + 4(\frac14) = \frac12 + 1 = \frac32 = 1.5$.

    They differ: $1.5 \neq 1$, and the difference $\mathbb{E}[X^2] - (\mathbb{E}[X])^2 = 0.5$ is exactly $\operatorname{var}(X)$ by Property 2 — which is the general reason $g(x)=x^2$ can never be pushed through an expectation unless the variable is constant.

    3.4 Conditional PMFs and conditional expectation

    Conditioning was introduced in G1 for events. Nothing new is needed here: a conditional PMF is an ordinary PMF computed in the reduced universe where the conditioning event is known to have occurred.

    Definition (conditional PMF and conditional expectation — L06 slide 5; B&T §2.6). Let $A$ be an event with $\mathbf{P}(A) > 0$. The conditional PMF of $X$ given $A$ is $$p_{X|A}(x) \;=\; \mathbf{P}(X = x \mid A) \;=\; \frac{\mathbf{P}\big(\{X=x\} \cap A\big)}{\mathbf{P}(A)},$$ the second equality being the definition of conditional probability from G1. The conditional expectation is the ordinary expectation computed with this PMF: $$\mathbb{E}[X \mid A] \;=\; \sum_x x\, p_{X|A}(x), \qquad\text{and more generally}\qquad \mathbb{E}[g(X)\mid A] = \sum_x g(x)\,p_{X|A}(x).$$ The conditional variance is likewise $\operatorname{var}(X \mid A) = \mathbb{E}\big[(X - \mathbb{E}[X\mid A])^2 \mid A\big] = \mathbb{E}[X^2 \mid A] - \big(\mathbb{E}[X\mid A]\big)^2$.
    Property (a conditional PMF really is a PMF). $p_{X|A}(x) \ge 0$ for all $x$ and $\sum_x p_{X|A}(x) = 1$.
    Proof

    Nonnegativity is immediate: the numerator $\mathbf{P}(\{X=x\}\cap A)$ and denominator $\mathbf{P}(A)$ are both nonnegative and the denominator is strictly positive. For normalization, the events $\{X = x\} \cap A$, as $x$ ranges over the values of $X$, are disjoint (different $x$'s cannot both occur) and their union is $A$ (whatever happens, $X$ takes some value). So by additivity, $$\sum_x \mathbf{P}\big(\{X=x\}\cap A\big) = \mathbf{P}(A),$$ and dividing both sides by $\mathbf{P}(A)$, $$\sum_x p_{X|A}(x) = \frac{\mathbf{P}(A)}{\mathbf{P}(A)} = 1. \qquad\blacksquare$$

    The practical content: conditioning is "delete and rescale". Delete the mass sitting outside $A$; multiply every surviving bar by the same factor $1/\mathbf{P}(A)$ so the total is 1 again. The shape of the surviving part is untouched.

    Example 3.3 (L06 slide 5 — the two blanks, solved). $X$ is uniform on $\{1,2,3,4\}$, i.e. $p_X(x) = 1/4$ for $x \in \{1,2,3,4\}$ and $0$ otherwise. Let $A = \{X \ge 2\}$. Find $p_{X|A}(x)$ and $\mathbb{E}[X\mid A]$.

    Step 1 — the conditioning probability. $A$ is the event that $X$ takes one of the values $2,3,4$. These are disjoint outcomes, so $$\mathbf{P}(A) = p_X(2)+p_X(3)+p_X(4) = \tfrac14+\tfrac14+\tfrac14 = \tfrac34.$$

    Step 2 — the conditional PMF. For $x \in \{2,3,4\}$ the event $\{X=x\}$ is contained in $A$, so $\{X=x\}\cap A = \{X=x\}$ and $$p_{X|A}(x) = \frac{p_X(x)}{\mathbf{P}(A)} = \frac{1/4}{3/4} = \frac13.$$ For $x = 1$ the event $\{X=1\}$ is disjoint from $A$, so the numerator is $0$ and $p_{X|A}(1) = 0$. Hence $$p_{X|A}(x) = \begin{cases} 1/3, & x \in \{2,3,4\},\\ 0, & \text{otherwise.}\end{cases}$$ Check: $1/3+1/3+1/3 = 1$. ✓ Conditioning on $A$ turned a uniform on four values into a uniform on three.

    Step 3 — the conditional expectation. $$\mathbb{E}[X\mid A] = \sum_x x\,p_{X|A}(x) = 2\cdot\tfrac13 + 3\cdot\tfrac13 + 4\cdot\tfrac13 = \tfrac{2+3+4}{3} = \tfrac93 = 3.$$ Compare with the unconditional $\mathbb{E}[X] = 5/2 = 2.5$: learning that $X$ is at least 2 pushes the mean up by $1/2$, as it must, since the conditioning removed the smallest value.

    Step 4 (bonus) — the conditional variance. $\mathbb{E}[X^2\mid A] = \frac{4+9+16}{3} = \frac{29}{3}$, so $$\operatorname{var}(X\mid A) = \tfrac{29}{3} - 3^2 = \tfrac{29}{3} - \tfrac{27}{3} = \tfrac23 \approx 0.666667,$$ smaller than the unconditional $\operatorname{var}(X) = 5/4 = 1.25$ — information narrowed the distribution. (computes/g2_s3.py keys C_*.)

    Fig. 3.3 — Conditioning is "delete and rescale" (L06 slide 5). The three surviving bars keep their relative sizes; all that changes is the common scale factor $1/\mathbf{P}(A) = 4/3$.
    Interpretation. Everything you know about PMFs transfers verbatim to conditional PMFs — normalization, the expected-value rule, the variance formula, linearity — because $p_{X|A}$ is a PMF, just one describing a smaller universe. This is why the course never needs a separate theory of "conditional expectation": it is the same theory run inside a shrunken sample space. The only thing that is genuinely new is how to stitch the shrunken universes back together, which is §3.6.
    Gotcha — conditioning can widen the spread, not just narrow it. It is tempting to think conditioning always shrinks the variance; it does in Example 3.3, but not in general. Conditioning on $A = \{X \in \{1,4\}\}$ for the same uniform $X$ gives a conditional PMF with mass $1/2$ at each of $1$ and $4$, hence $\mathbb{E}[X\mid A] = 2.5$ — the mean is unchanged — while $\operatorname{var}(X\mid A) = (1.5)^2 = 2.25 > 1.25$: keeping only the two extreme values hollowed out the middle. (The mean is equally free to move either way: conditioning on $\{X\ge2\}$ raised it to $3$, conditioning on $\{X\le2\}$ would lower it to $1.5$.) Conditioning reshapes the distribution; whether it narrows or widens depends entirely on which piece you kept.
    Practice 3.6. $X$ is uniform on $\{1,2,\dots,6\}$ (a fair die). Let $A = \{X \text{ is even}\}$. Find $p_{X|A}$, $\mathbb{E}[X\mid A]$, and $\operatorname{var}(X\mid A)$.
    Solution

    $\mathbf{P}(A) = \mathbf{P}(X\in\{2,4,6\}) = 3/6 = 1/2$. For $x \in \{2,4,6\}$, $p_{X|A}(x) = (1/6)/(1/2) = 1/3$; otherwise $0$.

    $\mathbb{E}[X\mid A] = \frac{2+4+6}{3} = 4$. $\mathbb{E}[X^2\mid A] = \frac{4+16+36}{3} = \frac{56}{3}$, so $\operatorname{var}(X\mid A) = \frac{56}{3} - 16 = \frac{56-48}{3} = \frac83 \approx 2.666667$.

    (Compare: unconditionally $\mathbb{E}[X]=3.5$ and $\operatorname{var}(X) = 91/6 - 49/4 = 35/12 \approx 2.916667$. Here conditioning raised the mean and slightly lowered the variance.)

    Practice 3.7. $X$ has $p_X(0) = 0.5$, $p_X(1) = 0.3$, $p_X(5) = 0.2$. Let $A = \{X \ge 1\}$. Find $\mathbb{E}[X \mid A]$, and then verify the identity $\mathbb{E}[X] = \mathbf{P}(A^c)\mathbb{E}[X\mid A^c] + \mathbf{P}(A)\mathbb{E}[X\mid A]$.
    Solution

    $\mathbf{P}(A) = 0.3 + 0.2 = 0.5$, so $p_{X|A}(1) = 0.3/0.5 = 0.6$ and $p_{X|A}(5) = 0.2/0.5 = 0.4$; these sum to 1. ✓

    $\mathbb{E}[X\mid A] = 1(0.6) + 5(0.4) = 0.6 + 2.0 = 2.6$.

    $A^c = \{X = 0\}$ has $\mathbf{P}(A^c) = 0.5$ and $\mathbb{E}[X\mid A^c] = 0$ (the only value there is $0$).

    Right-hand side: $0.5(0) + 0.5(2.6) = 1.3$. Direct: $\mathbb{E}[X] = 0(0.5)+1(0.3)+5(0.2) = 0.3+1.0 = 1.3$. ✓ This identity is the total expectation theorem of §3.6 in its two-piece form.

    3.5 The geometric PMF and memorylessness

    Definition (geometric random variable — L06 slide 6; B&T §2.2 for the PMF, with the mean and variance derived in B&T §2.6, B&T Example 2.17). Toss a coin repeatedly and independently, each toss coming up heads with probability $p \in (0,1]$. Let $X$ be the number of tosses up to and including the first head. Then $$p_X(k) \;=\; (1-p)^{k-1}\,p, \qquad k = 1, 2, 3, \ldots$$ Here $k$ is the index of the toss on which the first head occurs, $p$ is the per-toss success probability, and $1-p$ (often written $q$) is the per-toss failure probability. We say $X$ is geometric with parameter $p$.
    Where the formula comes from, and why it is a legitimate PMF

    The formula. The event $\{X = k\}$ is the single specific sequence "tail, tail, …, tail (a total of $k-1$ times), then head". The tosses are independent, so the probability of the sequence is the product of the per-toss probabilities: $$\mathbf{P}(X = k) = \underbrace{(1-p)(1-p)\cdots(1-p)}_{k-1 \text{ tails}} \cdot p = (1-p)^{k-1}p.$$

    Normalization. Sum over all $k \ge 1$ and substitute $q = 1-p$, so $0 \le q < 1$: $$\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} (1-p)^{k-1} p = p\sum_{k=1}^{\infty} q^{k-1} = p \sum_{j=0}^{\infty} q^{j} = p\cdot\frac{1}{1-q} = \frac{p}{p} = 1,$$ where the third equality re-indexes with $j = k-1$ and the fourth is the geometric series $\sum_{j\ge0} q^j = 1/(1-q)$, valid because $|q| < 1$. Verified numerically to 12 decimals for $p = 1/2, 1/5, 3/10$ (computes/g2_s3.py keys D_p=*_pmf_total_numeric).

    The tail probability, needed constantly below: $$\mathbf{P}(X > k) = \sum_{n = k+1}^{\infty}(1-p)^{n-1}p = p\, q^{k}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty} q^{j} = p\,q^{k}\cdot\frac{1}{1-q} = p\,q^k\cdot\frac1p = q^k = (1-p)^k.$$ (Re-index with $n = k+1+j$ so that $q^{n-1} = q^{k+j}$, pull out $q^k$, sum the series.) There is a shorter argument that gives the same thing in one line: $X > k$ means precisely that the first $k$ tosses were all tails, and by independence that has probability $(1-p)^k$.

    Theorem (memorylessness — L06 slide 6). Let $X$ be geometric with parameter $p$ and let $k \ge 0$ be an integer. Conditioned on $\{X > k\}$, the remaining number of tosses $X - k$ is again geometric with the same parameter $p$: $$\mathbf{P}\big(X - k = n \;\big|\; X > k\big) \;=\; p_X(n) \;=\; (1-p)^{n-1}p, \qquad n = 1,2,\ldots$$ Equivalently, $\mathbb{E}[X \mid X > k] = k + \mathbb{E}[X] = k + 1/p$.
    Derivation — cleanly, every step (the slide only draws the picture)

    Step 1 — name the two events. Fix an integer $n \ge 1$. The event of interest is $\{X - k = n\}$, i.e. $\{X = k+n\}$: the first head lands exactly $n$ tosses after the $k$ we have already watched fail. The conditioning event is $\{X > k\}$: the first $k$ tosses were all tails.

    Step 2 — the intersection. If $X = k+n$ with $n \ge 1$, then $X > k$ automatically. So $$\{X = k+n\} \cap \{X > k\} = \{X = k+n\},$$ and the joint probability is just $p_X(k+n)$.

    Step 3 — apply the definition of conditional probability. $$\mathbf{P}(X - k = n \mid X > k) \;=\; \frac{\mathbf{P}\big(\{X = k+n\}\cap\{X > k\}\big)}{\mathbf{P}(X > k)} \;=\; \frac{p_X(k+n)}{\mathbf{P}(X > k)}.$$

    Step 4 — substitute both pieces. The numerator is $p_X(k+n) = (1-p)^{(k+n)-1}p = (1-p)^{k+n-1}p$. The denominator is $\mathbf{P}(X>k) = (1-p)^k$, computed above. Therefore $$\mathbf{P}(X-k = n \mid X > k) \;=\; \frac{(1-p)^{k+n-1}\,p}{(1-p)^{k}}.$$

    Step 5 — cancel. Subtract exponents on the common base $(1-p)$: $$\frac{(1-p)^{k+n-1}}{(1-p)^{k}} = (1-p)^{(k+n-1)-k} = (1-p)^{n-1},$$ so $$\mathbf{P}(X-k = n \mid X > k) \;=\; (1-p)^{n-1}p \;=\; p_X(n).$$ The $k$ has vanished entirely. $\blacksquare$

    Step 6 — the expectation form. Because $X-k$ given $\{X>k\}$ has the same PMF as $X$, it has the same mean: $$\mathbb{E}[X - k \mid X > k] = \mathbb{E}[X] \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \mathbb{E}[X\mid X>k] = k + \mathbb{E}[X].$$ The first implication uses linearity inside the conditional universe: $\mathbb{E}[X-k \mid A] = \mathbb{E}[X\mid A] - k$ for the constant $k$. With $\mathbb{E}[X] = 1/p$ (proved in §3.7), $\mathbb{E}[X\mid X>k] = k + 1/p$.

    Exact verification. computes/g2_s3.py (keys D2_*) checks the identity in exact rational arithmetic for $p \in \{1/3, 1/5\}$, $k \in \{2,5\}$ and $n = 1,\dots,11$: zero mismatches.

    Fig. 3.4 — Memorylessness of the geometric PMF (L06 slide 6, three-panel figure), extended with the tail (e) and the parameter dependence (f). Panels (a)→(b)→(c) are exactly the slide's three plots: renormalize, then shift back, and you recover the original.
    Interpretation — the coin has no memory, and neither should you. Suppose you have tossed 10 tails in a row while waiting for a head. Memorylessness says the number of additional tosses you still need has exactly the same distribution as it had before you started — the ten wasted tosses bought you nothing and cost you nothing, informationally. This follows from independence of the tosses, but it is worth stating separately because it is the engine behind the one-line derivation of $\mathbb{E}[X] = 1/p$ in §3.7, and because it is the discrete cousin of the exponential distribution's memorylessness later in the course (B&T §3.3, Example 3.13). The geometric is the only discrete distribution on $\{1,2,\ldots\}$ with this property.
    Gotcha — memorylessness is not "the coin is due for a head". The gambler's fallacy says a long run of tails makes a head more likely next toss. Memorylessness says the opposite of that: the conditional probability of a head on the next toss is still $p$, and the conditional expected wait is still $1/p$ more tosses. Nothing is "due". What memorylessness does not say is that $\mathbb{E}[X \mid X>k] = \mathbb{E}[X]$ — the total wait $X$ certainly grows, to $k + 1/p$; it is only the remaining wait that is unchanged.
    Gotcha — two conventions for "geometric". This course counts the trial on which the first success occurs, so $X \in \{1,2,\ldots\}$, $\mathbb{E}[X]=1/p$. Many texts instead define the geometric as the number of failures before the first success, giving $Y = X - 1 \in \{0,1,2,\ldots\}$ with $\mathbb{E}[Y] = 1/p - 1 = (1-p)/p$. (The two standard Python routines both follow this course's convention, not that one: scipy.stats.geom has $\texttt{pmf}(0,p)=0$, $\texttt{pmf}(1,p)=p$ and mean $1/p$, and numpy.random.geometric returns values $\ge 1$ with sample mean $\approx 1/p$. To get the failures-only version you must shift deliberately — scipy.stats.geom(p, loc=-1), or numpy.random.negative_binomial(1, p).) The variances agree — $\operatorname{var}(Y) = \operatorname{var}(X-1) = \operatorname{var}(X) = (1-p)/p^2$ by §3.2 Property 1 — but the means differ by exactly 1. Always check which convention a formula assumes.

    The widget below is the numerical form of the theorem: it draws the original PMF $p_X(n)$ and, on top of it, the conditional PMF of the remaining tosses $X-k$ after $k$ observed failures. Move $k$ and watch nothing happen. (The identity was verified in exact and floating-point arithmetic in computes/g2_s3.py, keys H_*: maximum absolute difference below $10^{-15}$ for every $p$ and $k$ tested.)

    Practice 3.8. A component fails on any given day with probability $p = 0.02$, independently across days; $X$ is the day of failure. (a) What is $\mathbf{P}(X > 30)$? (b) Given that it has survived 30 days, what is the expected number of additional days until failure? (c) What is $\mathbb{E}[X \mid X > 30]$?
    Solution

    (a) $\mathbf{P}(X>30) = (1-0.02)^{30} = 0.98^{30} \approx 0.545484$.

    (b) By memorylessness the remaining wait is geometric with the same $p$, so its mean is $1/p = 1/0.02 = 50$ days. The 30 days of survival are irrelevant.

    (c) $\mathbb{E}[X\mid X>30] = 30 + 1/p = 30 + 50 = 80$ days.

    Note the contrast with (b): the total lifetime conditional mean grows with the elapsed time, the remaining lifetime conditional mean does not. Both statements are the same theorem.

    Practice 3.9. $X$ is geometric with parameter $p$. Show that $\mathbf{P}(X \text{ is even}) = \dfrac{1-p}{2-p}$, and evaluate it for $p = 1/2$ and $p = 1/4$.
    Solution

    Write $q = 1-p$. The even values are $k = 2, 4, 6, \dots$, i.e. $k = 2j$ for $j \ge 1$: $$\mathbf{P}(X \text{ even}) = \sum_{j=1}^{\infty} p_X(2j) = \sum_{j=1}^{\infty} q^{2j-1}p = p\,q\sum_{j=1}^{\infty} q^{2j-2} = p\,q\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(q^2)^{i} = \frac{p\,q}{1-q^2},$$ re-indexing with $i = j-1$ and summing the geometric series in ratio $q^2 < 1$. Now factor $1-q^2 = (1-q)(1+q) = p(1+q)$: $$\mathbf{P}(X\text{ even}) = \frac{p\,q}{p(1+q)} = \frac{q}{1+q} = \frac{1-p}{1+(1-p)} = \frac{1-p}{2-p}.$$

    For $p = 1/2$: $\frac{1/2}{3/2} = \frac13 \approx 0.333333$. For $p = 1/4$: $\frac{3/4}{7/4} = \frac37 \approx 0.428571$. Both confirmed by direct numerical summation (computes/g2_s3.py keys I4_*). Sanity: an even first success requires at least one failure first, so the answer must be below $1/2$, and it is.

    3.6 The total expectation theorem

    Conditioning (§3.4) breaks a problem into easier universes. The total expectation theorem is how you put the pieces back together. Recall from G1 that events $A_1,\dots,A_n$ form a partition of the sample space $\Omega$ when they are pairwise disjoint ($A_i \cap A_j = \varnothing$ for $i \neq j$) and collectively exhaustive ($A_1 \cup \cdots \cup A_n = \Omega$); we also assume $\mathbf{P}(A_i) > 0$ for each $i$ so the conditioning is defined.

    Theorem (total probability / total expectation — L06 slide 7; B&T §2.6). Let $A_1,\dots,A_n$ partition $\Omega$. Then for any event $B$ and any discrete random variable $X$: $$\mathbf{P}(B) = \sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{P}(A_i)\,\mathbf{P}(B\mid A_i), \tag{TP}$$ $$p_X(x) = \sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{P}(A_i)\,p_{X|A_i}(x) \quad\text{for every } x, \tag{TP-PMF}$$ $$\mathbb{E}[X] = \sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{P}(A_i)\,\mathbb{E}[X\mid A_i]. \tag{TE}$$
    Derivation of all three — the slide states them without proof

    (TP), the total probability theorem (G1). Because the $A_i$ partition $\Omega$, the sets $B\cap A_1, \dots, B\cap A_n$ are pairwise disjoint and their union is $B$. Additivity gives $$\mathbf{P}(B) = \sum_{i=1}^n \mathbf{P}(B\cap A_i) = \sum_{i=1}^n \mathbf{P}(A_i)\mathbf{P}(B\mid A_i),$$ the last step being the multiplication rule $\mathbf{P}(B\cap A_i) = \mathbf{P}(A_i)\mathbf{P}(B\mid A_i)$.

    (TP-PMF).
