Perfectly aligned variables ($X=Y$) reinforce: deviations always point the same way, so the sum swings twice as far and the variance quadruples. Perfectly opposed variables ($X=-Y$) cancel exactly, and the sum is deterministic. Independence sits exactly in the middle, where deviations neither reinforce nor cancel on average and the variances simply add. This is why averaging $n$ independent measurements reduces variance by $1/n$ but averaging $n$ copies of the same measurement reduces nothing.
    Gotcha. $\operatorname{var}(X-Y)=\operatorname{var}(X)+\operatorname{var}(Y)$ for independent $X,Y$ — you add, never subtract. Subtracting could produce a negative number, which no variance can be. Also note the asymmetry with expectation: $\mathbb{E}[X-Y]=\mathbb{E}[X]-\mathbb{E}[Y]$ always, but variance ignores the sign.
    Practice 4.14. $D_1,D_2,D_3$ are independent fair 6-sided dice. Find $\operatorname{var}(D_1+D_2+D_3)$ and $\operatorname{var}(D_1-D_2)$.
    Solution

    For one fair die, $\mathbb{E}[D]=\tfrac{1+2+3+4+5+6}{6}=\tfrac{21}{6}=3.5$ and $\mathbb{E}[D^2]=\tfrac{1+4+9+16+25+36}{6}=\tfrac{91}{6}$, so $\operatorname{var}(D)=\tfrac{91}{6}-(3.5)^2=\tfrac{91}{6}-\tfrac{49}{4} =\tfrac{182-147}{12}=\tfrac{35}{12}\approx2.9167$. By independence and additivity, $\operatorname{var}(D_1+D_2+D_3)=3\cdot\tfrac{35}{12}=\tfrac{35}{4}=8.75$ and $\operatorname{var}(D_1-D_2)=\operatorname{var}(D_1)+\operatorname{var}(D_2) =2\cdot\tfrac{35}{12}=\tfrac{35}{6}\approx5.8333$.

    Practice 4.15. Independent $X,Y$ have $\operatorname{var}(X)=4$, $\operatorname{var}(Y)=9$. For which constant $c$ is $\operatorname{var}(X+cY)$ smallest, and what is the minimum?
    Solution

    $\operatorname{var}(X+cY)=\operatorname{var}(X)+c^2\operatorname{var}(Y)=4+9c^2$. This is minimized at $c=0$, giving $4$: with independent noise, mixing in any multiple of $Y$ can only increase the spread. (If $X$ and $Y$ were correlated the answer would change — that is the idea behind least-squares estimation, developed later in the course.)

    4.8 Binomial mean and variance via indicator variables

    L07 slide 6 sets up the binomial mean as the brute-force sum $\mathbb{E}[X]=\sum_{k=0}^{n}k\binom{n}{k}p^k(1-p)^{n-k}$, introduces the indicator variables $X_i$, and then leaves every subsequent line blank. Filling them in is the payoff for §4.6–§4.7.

    Setup (L07 slide 6). $X$ is the number of successes in $n$ independent trials, each with success probability $p$; so $X$ is binomial with parameters $n$ and $p$. Define the indicator (also called Bernoulli) random variables $$X_i=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if trial }i\text{ is a success},\\ 0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases} \qquad i=1,\dots,n .$$ Since the trials are independent with common success probability, $X_1,\dots,X_n$ are independent and each has $\mathbf{P}(X_i=1)=p$, $\mathbf{P}(X_i=0)=1-p$.
    Fig. 4.4 — The indicator decomposition $X=X_1+\cdots+X_n$ (L07 slide 6). The mean needs only linearity; the variance additionally needs independence.
    Example 4.8 (L07 slide 6 — all four blanks filled).

    Step 0 — the key identity $X=\sum_{i=1}^n X_i$. For any outcome of the $n$ trials, the sum $X_1+\cdots+X_n$ adds a $1$ for each success and a $0$ for each failure, so it equals the number of successes, which is $X$. This is an identity between random variables (it holds outcome by outcome), not merely between their distributions.

    Blank 1 — $\mathbb{E}[X_i]$. Directly from the definition of expectation for a two-valued random variable: $$\mathbb{E}[X_i]=1\cdot\mathbf{P}(X_i=1)+0\cdot\mathbf{P}(X_i=0)=1\cdot p+0\cdot(1-p)=p .$$ The expectation of an indicator is the probability of the event it indicates — remember this; it is the engine of the whole method.

    Blank 2 — $\mathbb{E}[X]$. Apply linearity of expectation (§4.6) to the sum of $n$ terms: $$\mathbb{E}[X]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^n X_i\Big]=\sum_{i=1}^n\mathbb{E}[X_i] =\underbrace{p+p+\cdots+p}_{n\text{ terms}}=np .$$ Two lines, and no binomial coefficient in sight. Contrast the brute-force route on the slide, which requires the identity $k\binom{n}{k}=n\binom{n-1}{k-1}$ and a re-indexing of the sum. Note also that independence was never used — linearity holds regardless, which is precisely why the same argument will solve the hat problem of §4.10, where the $X_i$ are dependent.

    Blank 3 — $\operatorname{var}(X_i)$. Since $X_i$ only takes values $0$ and $1$, we have $X_i^2=X_i$ (because $0^2=0$ and $1^2=1$), hence $\mathbb{E}[X_i^2]=\mathbb{E}[X_i]=p$. Then $$\operatorname{var}(X_i)=\mathbb{E}[X_i^2]-(\mathbb{E}[X_i])^2=p-p^2=p(1-p).$$

    Blank 4 — $\operatorname{var}(X)$. The $X_i$ are independent, so variances add (§4.7), term by term: $$\operatorname{var}(X)=\operatorname{var}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^n X_i\Big)=\sum_{i=1}^n\operatorname{var}(X_i) =\underbrace{p(1-p)+\cdots+p(1-p)}_{n\text{ terms}}=np(1-p).$$

    Numerical check ($n=10$, $p=0.3$). The formulas give $\mathbb{E}[X]=10(0.3)=3$ and $\operatorname{var}(X)=10(0.3)(0.7)=2.1$, with $\operatorname{var}(X_i)=0.3\times0.7=0.21$. Evaluating the brute-force sums directly from the binomial PMF gives $\sum_{k=0}^{10}k\binom{10}{k}(0.3)^k(0.7)^{10-k}=3$ and $\sum_k k^2\binom{10}{k}(0.3)^k(0.7)^{10-k}-3^2=11.1-9=2.1$ — identical, as they must be.

    Recipe — the indicator method. When a random variable counts how many things of some kind happen:
    1. Name the events. $A_1,\dots,A_n$ = the individual things that might happen.
    2. Define indicators. $X_i=1$ if $A_i$ occurs, else $0$; then the count is $X=\sum_i X_i$ — verify this identity outcome by outcome.
    3. Mean. $\mathbb{E}[X]=\sum_i \mathbf{P}(A_i)$ by linearity. Works even when the $A_i$ overlap or are dependent.
    4. Variance. Only if the $X_i$ are independent may you write $\operatorname{var}(X)=\sum_i \mathbf{P}(A_i)(1-\mathbf{P}(A_i))$. Otherwise expand $X^2=\sum_i X_i^2+\sum_{i\ne j}X_iX_j$ and compute $\mathbb{E}[X_iX_j]=\mathbf{P}(A_i\cap A_j)$ pair by pair.
    Gotcha. Independence is needed for the variance step and only for the variance step. Students routinely under-use linearity (writing painful sums for means that need none) and over-use variance additivity (applying it to dependent indicators). Ask "am I adding expectations or variances?" before deciding whether independence matters.
    Practice 4.16. A fair coin is tossed $5$ times. Find the mean and variance of the number of heads, and $\mathbf{P}(X=5)$.
    Solution

    Binomial with $n=5$, $p=\tfrac12$: $\mathbb{E}[X]=np=5\cdot\tfrac12=\tfrac52=2.5$ and $\operatorname{var}(X)=np(1-p)=5\cdot\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac12=\tfrac54=1.25$. Also $\mathbf{P}(X=5)=\binom{5}{5}(\tfrac12)^5=\tfrac{1}{32}=0.03125$.

    Practice 4.17. Ten independent trials have success probabilities $p_1,\dots,p_{10}$ that are not all equal. Is $\mathbb{E}[X]=\sum_i p_i$ still correct? Is $\operatorname{var}(X)=\sum_i p_i(1-p_i)$?
    Solution

    Both are correct. $X=\sum_i X_i$ still holds, $\mathbb{E}[X_i]=p_i$, and linearity gives $\mathbb{E}[X]=\sum_i p_i$ with no assumption on the $p_i$ or on independence. The variance formula uses $\operatorname{var}(X_i)=p_i(1-p_i)$ and additivity, which requires independence — which we have here. ($X$ is no longer binomial; it is called Poisson-binomial. The mean and variance formulas survive, the PMF does not.)

    Practice 4.18. Use indicators to find the expected number of pairs $(i,j)$, $i<j$, of the $n=5$ tosses in Practice 4.16 that are both heads.
    Solution

    Index the $\binom52=10$ pairs and let $Z_{ij}=1$ if tosses $i$ and $j$ are both heads. Then $\mathbb{E}[Z_{ij}]=\mathbf{P}(\text{both heads})=\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac12=\tfrac14$ by independence of the two tosses. By linearity the expected number of all-heads pairs is $10\cdot\tfrac14=\tfrac{10}{4}=2.5$. Note that the $Z_{ij}$ are dependent (they share tosses) — irrelevant for the mean.

    4.9 Recitation workout: three joint-PMF problems in full

    Problem 4.A (rec06 P1) — a four-sided die decides how many coins to flip. A fair four-sided die with faces labeled $0,1,2,3$ is thrown once to determine how many times a fair coin is to be flipped. Define $N$ = the result of the die roll and $K$ = the total number of heads obtained. (a) Determine and sketch $p_N(n)$. (b) Determine and tabulate $p_{N,K}(n,k)$. (c) Determine and sketch $p_{K|N}(k\mid2)$. (d) Determine and sketch $p_{N|K}(n\mid2)$.
    Solution

    (a) The marginal of $N$. The die is fair with four faces, so each of the outcomes $0,1,2,3$ has probability $\tfrac14$: $$p_N(n)=\begin{cases}\tfrac14,&n\in\{0,1,2,3\},\\ 0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}$$ This needs nothing but the die (left panel of Fig. 4.5 shows the joint table; the marginal is the row totals, each $\tfrac14$). Its mean and variance: $\mathbb{E}[N]=\tfrac{0+1+2+3}{4}=\tfrac{6}{4}=\tfrac32=1.5$, and $\mathbb{E}[N^2]=\tfrac{0+1+4+9}{4}=\tfrac{14}{4}=3.5$, so $\operatorname{var}(N)=3.5-(1.5)^2=3.5-2.25=1.25=\tfrac54$.

    (b) The joint PMF. Use the multiplication rule $p_{N,K}(n,k)=p_N(n)\,p_{K|N}(k\mid n)$. Given $N=n$, the coin is flipped $n$ independent times with $\mathbf{P}(H)=\tfrac12$, so conditionally $K$ is binomial with parameters $n$ and $\tfrac12$: $$p_{K|N}(k\mid n)=\binom{n}{k}\Big(\tfrac12\Big)^k\Big(\tfrac12\Big)^{n-k} =\frac{\binom{n}{k}}{2^{\,n}},\qquad k=0,1,\dots,n .$$ Multiplying by $p_N(n)=\tfrac14$ throughout:

    $n=0$ (no flips at all, so $K=0$ with certainty): $p_{N,K}(0,0)=\tfrac14\cdot1=\tfrac14$.
    $n=1$: $\binom10/2=\tfrac12$ and $\binom11/2=\tfrac12$, so $p_{N,K}(1,0)=\tfrac14\cdot\tfrac12=\tfrac18$ and $p_{N,K}(1,1)=\tfrac18$.
    $n=2$: $\tfrac{\binom20}{4}=\tfrac14$, $\tfrac{\binom21}{4}=\tfrac24=\tfrac12$, $\tfrac{\binom22}{4}=\tfrac14$, so $p_{N,K}(2,0)=\tfrac14\cdot\tfrac14=\tfrac1{16}$, $p_{N,K}(2,1)=\tfrac14\cdot\tfrac12=\tfrac18$, $p_{N,K}(2,2)=\tfrac1{16}$.
    $n=3$: $\tfrac{\binom30}{8}=\tfrac18$, $\tfrac{\binom31}{8}=\tfrac38$, $\tfrac{\binom32}{8}=\tfrac38$, $\tfrac{\binom33}{8}=\tfrac18$, so $p_{N,K}(3,0)=\tfrac1{32}$, $p_{N,K}(3,1)=\tfrac3{32}$, $p_{N,K}(3,2)=\tfrac3{32}$, $p_{N,K}(3,3)=\tfrac1{32}$.

    $$ \begin{array}{c|cccc} p_{N,K}(n,k) & k=0 & k=1 & k=2 & k=3\\\hline n=0 & 1/4 & 0 & 0 & 0\\ n=1 & 1/8 & 1/8 & 0 & 0\\ n=2 & 1/16 & 1/8 & 1/16 & 0\\ n=3 & 1/32 & 3/32 & 3/32 & 1/32 \end{array} $$

    Normalization check over the common denominator $32$: $8+(4+4)+(2+4+2)+(1+3+3+1)=8+8+8+8=32$, i.e. the entries sum to $\tfrac{32}{32}=1$ ✓. (Each row sums to $\tfrac14$, as it must, since the row total is $p_N(n)$.)

    (c) $p_{K|N}(k\mid2)$. Divide row $n=2$ by $p_N(2)=\tfrac14$ — equivalently, read off the conditional binomial with $n=2$, $p=\tfrac12$: $$p_{K|N}(0\mid2)=\frac{1/16}{1/4}=\frac14,\qquad p_{K|N}(1\mid2)=\frac{1/8}{1/4}=\frac12,\qquad p_{K|N}(2\mid2)=\frac{1/16}{1/4}=\frac14,$$ and $0$ otherwise; the three add to $\tfrac14+\tfrac12+\tfrac14=1$ ✓ (middle panel of Fig. 4.5). Its mean is $\mathbb{E}[K\mid N=2]=0(\tfrac14)+1(\tfrac12)+2(\tfrac14)=1$, consistent with the binomial formula $np=2\cdot\tfrac12=1$.

    (d) $p_{N|K}(n\mid2)$. This is the "reverse" conditional and needs Bayes-style arithmetic. First the marginal of $K$ at $2$ — sum the column $k=2$ (marginalization, §4.2): $$p_K(2)=p_{N,K}(0,2)+p_{N,K}(1,2)+p_{N,K}(2,2)+p_{N,K}(3,2) =0+0+\tfrac1{16}+\tfrac3{32}=\tfrac{2}{32}+\tfrac{3}{32}=\tfrac{5}{32}=0.15625 .$$ Only $n=2$ and $n=3$ contribute: to get $2$ heads you need at least $2$ tosses, and $N\le3$. Now divide: $$p_{N|K}(2\mid2)=\frac{p_{N,K}(2,2)}{p_K(2)}=\frac{1/16}{5/32} =\frac{2/32}{5/32}=\frac25=0.4,$$ $$p_{N|K}(3\mid2)=\frac{p_{N,K}(3,2)}{p_K(2)}=\frac{3/32}{5/32}=\frac35=0.6 .$$ (The recitation handout states $3/5$ without showing this division — [SOLUTION GAP] filled. The handout also contains two typos: it says "at least 3 coin tosses ... only $N=3$ and $N=4$", which should read "at least 2 coin tosses ... only $N=2$ and $N=3$", since $N\le3$ always; and it writes the denominator as the four-term sum $1/16+1/32+1/32+1/32$, which equals the correct $5/32$ because $3/32$ has been split into three $1/32$'s. The numerical answers are unaffected.) Check: $\tfrac25+\tfrac35=1$ ✓ (right panel of Fig. 4.5). The conditional mean is $\mathbb{E}[N\mid K=2]=2(\tfrac25)+3(\tfrac35)=\tfrac45+\tfrac95=\tfrac{13}{5}=2.6$ — seeing two heads makes the larger die value more plausible, pushing the mean above the unconditional $\mathbb{E}[N]=1.5$.

    Beyond the handout — the marginal of $K$, and $\mathbb{E}[K]$ two ways. Summing each column over the common denominator $32$: $$p_K(0)=\tfrac{8+4+2+1}{32}=\tfrac{15}{32},\quad p_K(1)=\tfrac{0+4+4+3}{32}=\tfrac{11}{32},\quad p_K(2)=\tfrac{5}{32},\quad p_K(3)=\tfrac{1}{32},$$ summing to $\tfrac{15+11+5+1}{32}=\tfrac{32}{32}=1$ ✓. Then directly, $$\mathbb{E}[K]=0\cdot\tfrac{15}{32}+1\cdot\tfrac{11}{32}+2\cdot\tfrac{5}{32}+3\cdot\tfrac{1}{32} =\tfrac{11+10+3}{32}=\tfrac{24}{32}=\tfrac34=0.75 .$$ Via the total expectation theorem instead, using $\mathbb{E}[K\mid N=n]=n\cdot\tfrac12$ (conditional binomial mean): $$\mathbb{E}[K]=\sum_{n=0}^{3}p_N(n)\,\mathbb{E}[K\mid N=n] =\tfrac14\Big(0+\tfrac12+1+\tfrac32\Big)=\tfrac14\cdot 3=\tfrac34=0.75\ ✓$$ — the same answer with far less arithmetic, and the reason conditioning is a computational tool and not just a concept. For completeness, $\mathbb{E}[K^2]=0+1\cdot\tfrac{11}{32}+4\cdot\tfrac{5}{32}+9\cdot\tfrac{1}{32}=\tfrac{40}{32}=\tfrac54$, so $\operatorname{var}(K)=\tfrac54-\big(\tfrac34\big)^2=\tfrac{20-9}{16}=\tfrac{11}{16}=0.6875$.

    Are $N$ and $K$ independent? No. $p_{N,K}(0,1)=0$ while $p_N(0)\,p_K(1)=\tfrac14\cdot\tfrac{11}{32}=\tfrac{11}{128}\approx0.0859\ne0$: the support is triangular, not rectangular (Recipe of §4.5, test 1).

    Fig. 4.5 — rec06 P1: the joint PMF $p_{N,K}$ (zero above the diagonal, since $K\le N$), and the two conditional PMFs requested in parts (c) and (d).
    Problem 4.B (rec06 P2) — eight equally likely points in the plane. The outcome space consists of eight equally likely points, each of probability $\tfrac18$, at $(0,3),(4,3),(2,2),(4,2),(0,1),(2,1),(4,1),(4,0)$, where the coordinates are the values of $(X,Y)$. (a) Which value(s) of $x$ maximize $\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=x]$? (b) Which value(s) of $y$ maximize $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=y)$? (c) Let $R=\min(X,Y)$; sketch $p_R(r)$. (d) Let $A$ be the event $X^2\ge Y$; find $\mathbb{E}[XY]$ and $\mathbb{E}[XY\mid A]$.
    Solution

    Marginals first. Group the eight points by $x$: $x=0$ has $(0,3),(0,1)$; $x=2$ has $(2,2),(2,1)$; $x=4$ has $(4,3),(4,2),(4,1),(4,0)$. Hence $$p_X(0)=\tfrac28=\tfrac14,\qquad p_X(2)=\tfrac28=\tfrac14,\qquad p_X(4)=\tfrac48=\tfrac12,$$ summing to $1$ ✓. Grouping by $y$: $y=0$ has $(4,0)$; $y=1$ has $(0,1),(2,1),(4,1)$; $y=2$ has $(2,2),(4,2)$; $y=3$ has $(0,3),(4,3)$, so $$p_Y(0)=\tfrac18,\qquad p_Y(1)=\tfrac38,\qquad p_Y(2)=\tfrac28=\tfrac14,\qquad p_Y(3)=\tfrac28=\tfrac14,$$ summing to $1$ ✓.

    (a) Conditional means of $Y$ (the handout states the three values with no arithmetic — [SOLUTION GAP] filled). Conditioning on $X=x$ makes the points in that column equally likely, since they had equal mass to begin with.

    • $X=0$: the surviving points are $(0,3),(0,1)$, each with conditional probability $\frac{1/8}{1/4}=\tfrac12$. So $\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=0]=3(\tfrac12)+1(\tfrac12)=\tfrac{3+1}{2}=2 .$
    • $X=2$: points $(2,2),(2,1)$, each $\tfrac12$, so $\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=2]=2(\tfrac12)+1(\tfrac12)=\tfrac32=1.5 .$
    • $X=4$: points $(4,3),(4,2),(4,1),(4,0)$, each $\frac{1/8}{1/2}=\tfrac14$, so $\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=4]=\tfrac{3+2+1+0}{4}=\tfrac64=\tfrac32=1.5 .$

    For any other $x$ the conditional expectation is undefined ($p_X(x)=0$). The maximum is $2$, attained uniquely at $\boxed{x=0}$.

    (b) Conditional variances of $X$ ([SOLUTION GAP] filled). Conditioning on $Y=y$ again leaves a uniform distribution on the surviving $x$-values.

    • $y=0$: only $(4,0)$, so $X=4$ with probability $1$; $\mathbb{E}[X\mid Y=0]=4$ and $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=0)=0$.
    • $y=1$: $X$ is uniform on $\{0,2,4\}$ (each $\frac{1/8}{3/8}=\tfrac13$). Mean $\tfrac{0+2+4}{3}=2$; second moment $\tfrac{0+4+16}{3}=\tfrac{20}{3}$; so $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=1)=\tfrac{20}{3}-2^2=\tfrac{20-12}{3}=\tfrac83\approx2.6667 .$
    • $y=2$: $X$ uniform on $\{2,4\}$ (each $\tfrac12$). Mean $3$; second moment $\tfrac{4+16}{2}=10$; so $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=2)=10-9=1 .$
    • $y=3$: $X$ uniform on $\{0,4\}$ (each $\tfrac12$). Mean $2$; second moment $\tfrac{0+16}{2}=8$; so $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=3)=8-4=4 .$

    The maximum is $4$, attained at $\boxed{y=3}$ — the two surviving points are as far apart as the diagram allows, which is what maximizes spread.

    (c) The PMF of $R=\min(X,Y)$ ([SOLUTION GAP]: the handout gives only the sketch; here is the point-by-point mapping). Evaluate $\min(x,y)$ at each of the eight equally likely points: $$(0,3)\mapsto0,\quad(0,1)\mapsto0,\quad(4,0)\mapsto0,\quad (2,1)\mapsto1,\quad(4,1)\mapsto1,\quad(2,2)\mapsto2,\quad(4,2)\mapsto2,\quad(4,3)\mapsto3 .$$ Counting: three points give $R=0$, two give $R=1$, two give $R=2$, one gives $R=3$. Since each point has mass $\tfrac18$, $$p_R(0)=\tfrac38,\qquad p_R(1)=\tfrac28=\tfrac14,\qquad p_R(2)=\tfrac28=\tfrac14,\qquad p_R(3)=\tfrac18,$$ summing to $\tfrac{3+2+2+1}{8}=1$ ✓ (right panel of Fig. 4.6). Its mean: $\mathbb{E}[R]=0(\tfrac38)+1(\tfrac14)+2(\tfrac14)+3(\tfrac18)=\tfrac{0+2+4+3}{8}=\tfrac98=1.125$. Note this is a function of two random variables handled by the expected value rule in disguise — we summed $g(x,y)=\min(x,y)$ against the joint PMF.

    (d) $\mathbb{E}[XY]$ and $\mathbb{E}[XY\mid A]$. By the expected value rule with $g(x,y)=xy$, traversing the points top to bottom and left to right: $$\mathbb{E}[XY]=\tfrac18\big(0\cdot3+4\cdot3+2\cdot2+4\cdot2+0\cdot1+2\cdot1+4\cdot1+4\cdot0\big) =\tfrac18\big(0+12+4+8+0+2+4+0\big)=\tfrac{30}{8}=\tfrac{15}{4}=3.75 .$$ For the conditional version, first identify $A=\{X^2\ge Y\}$ point by point: $(0,3)$: $0\ge3$ false; $(4,3)$: $16\ge3$ true; $(2,2)$: $4\ge2$ true; $(4,2)$: true; $(0,1)$: $0\ge1$ false; $(2,1)$: $4\ge1$ true; $(4,1)$: true; $(4,0)$: $16\ge0$ true. So conditioning on $A$ removes exactly the two point masses at $(0,3)$ and $(0,1)$, leaving six, and $$\mathbf{P}(A)=\tfrac68=\tfrac34=0.75,\qquad \text{each survivor now has mass } \frac{1/8}{3/4}=\frac16 .$$ Therefore $$\mathbb{E}[XY\mid A]=\tfrac16\big(4\cdot3+2\cdot2+4\cdot2+2\cdot1+4\cdot1+4\cdot0\big) =\tfrac16\big(12+4+8+2+4+0\big)=\tfrac{30}{6}=5 .$$ Conditioning raised the mean product from $3.75$ to $5$ — unsurprising, since the discarded points both had $X=0$ and hence $XY=0$.

    Bonus: are $X$ and $Y$ independent here? No. $\mathbb{E}[X]=0(\tfrac14)+2(\tfrac14)+4(\tfrac12)=\tfrac52$ and $\mathbb{E}[Y]=0(\tfrac18)+1(\tfrac38)+2(\tfrac14)+3(\tfrac14)=\tfrac{0+3+4+6}{8}=\tfrac{13}{8}$, so $\mathbb{E}[X]\mathbb{E}[Y]=\tfrac52\cdot\tfrac{13}{8}=\tfrac{65}{16}=4.0625$ while $\mathbb{E}[XY]=3.75$; the covariance is $3.75-4.0625=-0.3125=-\tfrac{5}{16}\ne0$, which proves dependence (§4.5). The support is also ragged — e.g. $(0,0)$ has probability $0$ although $p_X(0)>0$ and $p_Y(0)>0$.

    Fig. 4.6 — rec06 P2. Left: the eight equally likely points with the conditional means $\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=x]$ marked; $x=0$ is the maximizer. Right: the PMF of $R=\min(X,Y)$ from part (c).
    Problem 4.C (rec07 P2) — a dot-diagram joint PMF with unspecified entries. $X$ and $Y$ take values in $\{1,2,3\}$. The joint PMF is partially specified (entries marked $*$ are unknown): $$ \begin{array}{c|ccc} p_{X,Y}(x,y) & x=1 & x=2 & x=3\\\hline y=3 & 1/12 & 1/12 & *\\ y=2 & 2/12 & * & *\\ y=1 & 1/12 & 2/12 & 0 \end{array} $$ (a) What is $p_X(1)$? (b) Sketch the conditional PMF of $Y$ given $X=1$. (c) What is $\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=1]$? (d) Is there a choice of the unspecified entries making $X$ and $Y$ independent? Let $B=\{X\le2\ \text{and}\ Y\le2\}$, and suppose that conditioned on $B$, $X$ and $Y$ are independent. (e) What is $p_{X,Y}(2,2)$? (f) What is $p_{X,Y\mid B}(2,2)$?
    Solution

    (a) The whole column $x=1$ is known, so marginalization (§4.2) works despite the missing entries: $$p_X(1)=p_{X,Y}(1,1)+p_{X,Y}(1,2)+p_{X,Y}(1,3)=\tfrac{1}{12}+\tfrac{2}{12}+\tfrac{1}{12} =\tfrac{4}{12}=\tfrac13\approx0.3333 .$$

    (b) Divide that column by its total: $$p_{Y|X}(y\mid1)=\frac{p_{X,Y}(1,y)}{p_X(1)}:\qquad p_{Y|X}(1\mid1)=\frac{1/12}{4/12}=\frac14,\quad p_{Y|X}(2\mid1)=\frac{2/12}{4/12}=\frac12,\quad p_{Y|X}(3\mid1)=\frac{1/12}{4/12}=\frac14,$$ and $0$ otherwise; the three add to $1$ ✓. The handout gives only this formula and no drawing ([SOLUTION GAP]); the sketch is the right panel of Fig. 4.7.

    (c) $$\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=1]=\sum_{y=1}^{3}y\,p_{Y|X}(y\mid1) =1\cdot\tfrac14+2\cdot\tfrac12+3\cdot\tfrac14=\tfrac14+1+\tfrac34=2 .$$ (The conditional law is symmetric about $y=2$, so the answer had to be $2$.) For completeness, $\mathbb{E}[Y^2\mid X=1]=1(\tfrac14)+4(\tfrac12)+9(\tfrac14)=\tfrac{1+8+9}{4}=\tfrac{18}{4}=4.5$, so $\operatorname{var}(Y\mid X=1)=4.5-4=0.5$.

    (d) No choice makes them independent. (All the intermediate arithmetic the handout skips is shown — [SOLUTION GAP] filled.) Argue by contradiction: suppose $X,Y$ are independent. The bottom row is fully known, so $$p_Y(1)=p_{X,Y}(1,1)+p_{X,Y}(2,1)+p_{X,Y}(3,1)=\tfrac1{12}+\tfrac2{12}+0=\tfrac{3}{12}=\tfrac14 .$$ Since $p_{X,Y}(3,1)=0$ and independence requires $p_{X,Y}(3,1)=p_X(3)p_Y(1)$, we get $p_X(3)\cdot\tfrac14=0$, hence $p_X(3)=0$. A zero marginal forces the whole column to vanish: $p_{X,Y}(3,2)=p_{X,Y}(3,3)=0$. The only unspecified entry left is $(2,2)$, and it must absorb all remaining mass: $$p_{X,Y}(2,2)=1-\Big[\tfrac1{12}+\tfrac2{12}+\tfrac1{12}+\tfrac2{12}+\tfrac1{12}\Big] =1-\tfrac{7}{12}=\tfrac{5}{12},$$ the bracket being the five known nonzero entries $(1,1),(1,2),(1,3),(2,1),(2,3)$. Now compute the two marginals implied by this completed table: $$p_X(2)=p_{X,Y}(2,1)+p_{X,Y}(2,2)+p_{X,Y}(2,3)=\tfrac2{12}+\tfrac5{12}+\tfrac1{12}=\tfrac{8}{12}=\tfrac23,$$ $$p_Y(2)=p_{X,Y}(1,2)+p_{X,Y}(2,2)+p_{X,Y}(3,2)=\tfrac2{12}+\tfrac5{12}+0=\tfrac{7}{12}.$$ Independence would demand $$p_{X,Y}(2,2)=p_X(2)p_Y(2)=\tfrac{8}{12}\cdot\tfrac{7}{12}=\tfrac{56}{144}=\tfrac{7}{18}\approx0.3889,$$ but we just forced $p_{X,Y}(2,2)=\tfrac5{12}\approx0.4167$. Since $0.4167\ne0.3889$, the assumption fails: no completion makes $X$ and $Y$ independent.

    A shorter argument (also in the handout, with its ratios left unevaluated). Use only the four fully known cells at $x\in\{1,2\}$, $y\in\{1,3\}$. Independence would give $$\frac{p_{X,Y}(1,3)}{p_{X,Y}(1,1)}=\frac{p_X(1)p_Y(3)}{p_X(1)p_Y(1)}=\frac{p_Y(3)}{p_Y(1)} =\frac{p_X(2)p_Y(3)}{p_X(2)p_Y(1)}=\frac{p_{X,Y}(2,3)}{p_{X,Y}(2,1)} .$$ But $$\frac{p_{X,Y}(1,3)}{p_{X,Y}(1,1)}=\frac{1/12}{1/12}=1 \qquad\text{while}\qquad \frac{p_{X,Y}(2,3)}{p_{X,Y}(2,1)}=\frac{1/12}{2/12}=\frac12 ,$$ and $1\ne\tfrac12$. The columns are not proportional, so independence is impossible — no knowledge of the starred entries required. (This is test 2 of the Recipe in §4.5.)

    (e) $p_{X,Y}(2,2)$ from conditional independence given $B$. The event $B=\{X\le2,\ Y\le2\}$ contains exactly the four cells $(1,1),(2,1),(1,2),(2,2)$. Conditioned on $B$, the joint PMF is each of these divided by $\mathbf{P}(B)$, and conditional independence means that the four conditional cells factor. Equivalently — and this is the cleanest form because $\mathbf{P}(B)$ cancels — the $2\times2$ block of unconditional masses must have proportional rows: $$\frac{p_{X,Y}(1,1)}{p_{X,Y}(1,2)}=\frac{p_{X,Y}(2,1)}{p_{X,Y}(2,2)} .$$ (Both sides equal $p_{Y|B}(1)/p_{Y|B}(2)$ after dividing numerator and denominator by $\mathbf{P}(B)\,p_{X|B}(x)$.) Solve for the unknown: $$p_{X,Y}(2,2)=\frac{p_{X,Y}(1,2)\,p_{X,Y}(2,1)}{p_{X,Y}(1,1)} =\frac{(2/12)(2/12)}{1/12}=\frac{4/144}{1/12}=\frac{4}{144}\cdot\frac{12}{1}=\frac{4}{12}=\frac13 \approx0.3333 .$$

    (f) $p_{X,Y\mid B}(2,2)$. First $\mathbf{P}(B)$, by adding the four cells of the block ([SOLUTION GAP]: the handout asserts $9/12$ without this sum): $$\mathbf{P}(B)=p_{X,Y}(1,1)+p_{X,Y}(2,1)+p_{X,Y}(1,2)+p_{X,Y}(2,2) =\tfrac1{12}+\tfrac2{12}+\tfrac2{12}+\tfrac4{12}=\tfrac{9}{12}=\tfrac34=0.75 .$$ Then renormalize (§4.4): $$p_{X,Y\mid B}(2,2)=\frac{p_{X,Y}(2,2)}{\mathbf{P}(B)}=\frac{1/3}{3/4} =\frac13\cdot\frac43=\frac{4}{9}\approx0.4444 .$$ The other three conditional cells are $\frac{1/12}{3/4}=\tfrac19$, $\frac{2/12}{3/4}=\tfrac29$ and $\tfrac29$; they sum with $\tfrac49$ to $\tfrac{1+2+2+4}{9}=1$ ✓. As a final consistency check on (e), the conditional marginals are $p_{X\mid B}(1)=\tfrac19+\tfrac29=\tfrac13$, $p_{X\mid B}(2)=\tfrac29+\tfrac49=\tfrac23$, $p_{Y\mid B}(1)=\tfrac19+\tfrac29=\tfrac13$, $p_{Y\mid B}(2)=\tfrac23$, and indeed $p_{X\mid B}(2)p_{Y\mid B}(2)=\tfrac23\cdot\tfrac23=\tfrac49=p_{X,Y\mid B}(2,2)$ ✓ — the conditional independence we were told to assume.

    The moral. Parts (d) and (e)–(f) together show that unconditional dependence and conditional independence coexist happily in the same table: restricting to $B$ throws away the very cells (the $x=3$ column and the $y=3$ row) that broke the proportionality.

    Fig. 4.7 — rec07 P2. Left: the given dot diagram, $*$ marking unspecified masses. Right: the conditional PMF $p_{Y|X}(y\mid1)$ requested in part (b), with $\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=1]=2$ marked.
    Practice 4.19. In rec06 P1, compute $p_{N|K}(n\mid0)$ and $\mathbb{E}[N\mid K=0]$.
    Solution

    Column $k=0$ of the table has entries $\tfrac14,\tfrac18,\tfrac1{16},\tfrac1{32}$ at $n=0,1,2,3$; over the denominator $32$ these are $\tfrac{8}{32},\tfrac{4}{32},\tfrac{2}{32}, \tfrac{1}{32}$, so $p_K(0)=\tfrac{15}{32}$. Dividing: $$p_{N|K}(0\mid0)=\tfrac{8}{15},\quad p_{N|K}(1\mid0)=\tfrac{4}{15},\quad p_{N|K}(2\mid0)=\tfrac{2}{15},\quad p_{N|K}(3\mid0)=\tfrac{1}{15},$$ summing to $\tfrac{15}{15}=1$ ✓. Then $\mathbb{E}[N\mid K=0]=\tfrac{0\cdot8+1\cdot4+2\cdot2+3\cdot1}{15}=\tfrac{11}{15}\approx0.7333$ — well below the unconditional $\mathbb{E}[N]=1.5$, since seeing no heads makes few tosses more plausible.

    Practice 4.20. In rec06 P2, compute $\operatorname{var}(Y\mid X=4)$ and compare it with $\operatorname{var}(Y\mid X=0)$.
    Solution

    Given $X=4$, $Y$ is uniform on $\{0,1,2,3\}$ (each $\tfrac14$): mean $\tfrac{0+1+2+3}{4}=\tfrac32$, second moment $\tfrac{0+1+4+9}{4}=\tfrac{14}{4}=3.5$, so $\operatorname{var}(Y\mid X=4)=3.5-2.25=1.25=\tfrac54$. Given $X=0$, $Y$ is uniform on $\{1,3\}$: mean $2$, second moment $\tfrac{1+9}{2}=5$, so $\operatorname{var}(Y\mid X=0)=5-4=1$. The column $x=4$ is both lower on average and more spread out.

    Practice 4.21. In rec07 P2, suppose instead you are told the three starred entries are equal. Determine them.
    Solution

    The five known entries sum to $\tfrac{1+1+2+1+2}{12}=\tfrac{7}{12}$ (with $p_{X,Y}(3,1)=0$), so the three starred cells must total $1-\tfrac{7}{12}=\tfrac{5}{12}$. If they are equal, each is $\tfrac{5}{36}\approx0.1389$. Note this is not the value $\tfrac13$ found in part (e) for $p_{X,Y}(2,2)$, which is fine: (e) used a different piece of information (conditional independence given $B$), and a partially specified table can be completed in infinitely many ways.

    4.10 The hat problem: indicators without independence

    L07 closes with the problem the whole section has been building toward, and leaves every line of it blank across two slides. It is the exact stress test for §4.6–§4.8: the indicator decomposition still works, linearity still gives the mean for free, but the indicators are now dependent, so the variance can no longer be read off term by term. Every number below is recomputed — including a brute-force check over all $n!$ permutations for $n=2,3,5$ — in computes/g2_s4.py (block "hat problem").

    Setup (L07 slide 7). $n$ people throw their hats into a box; the hats are mixed and each person then draws one hat at random, so that the assignment of hats to people is a uniformly random permutation of the $n$ hats — all $n!$ orderings equally likely. Let $X$ be the number of people who get their own hat back, and define the indicators $$X_i=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if person }i\text{ selects their own hat},\\ 0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases} \qquad i=1,\dots,n,\qquad\text{so that}\qquad X=X_1+X_2+\cdots+X_n .$$ As in §4.8 the identity $X=\sum_i X_i$ holds outcome by outcome: each person contributes a $1$ to the count exactly when their own indicator is $1$.
    Example 4.9 (L07 slides 7–8 — all eight blanks filled).

    Blank 1 — $\mathbf{P}(X_i=1)$. Fix a person $i$. By symmetry every one of the $n$ hats is equally likely to be the one person $i$ draws — no hat is distinguished by the shuffling — and exactly one of them is person $i$'s own. The discrete uniform law of G1 therefore gives $$\mathbf{P}(X_i=1)=\frac{\#\{\text{favorable hats}\}}{\#\{\text{possible hats}\}}=\frac1n .$$ (Counting permutations gives the same answer: of the $n!$ equally likely permutations, exactly $(n-1)!$ fix person $i$, and $(n-1)!/n!=1/n$.) Consequently $\mathbf{P}(X_i=0)=1-\tfrac1n$.

    Blank 2 — $\mathbb{E}[X_i]$. $X_i$ is an indicator, so its expectation is the probability of the event it indicates (the rule isolated in §4.8): $$\mathbb{E}[X_i]=1\cdot\mathbf{P}(X_i=1)+0\cdot\mathbf{P}(X_i=0)=1\cdot\tfrac1n+0=\tfrac1n .$$

    Blank 3 — are the $X_i$ independent? No. Take $n\ge2$ and condition on person $1$ getting their own hat. That hat is gone, so person $2$ is drawing uniformly among the remaining $n-1$ hats, exactly one of which is person $2$'s own: $$\mathbf{P}(X_2=1\mid X_1=1)=\frac{1}{n-1}\ \ne\ \frac1n=\mathbf{P}(X_2=1)\qquad(n\ge2).$$ Since conditioning on $X_1$ changes the law of $X_2$, the factorization test of §4.5 fails and the indicators are dependent. The dependence is positive here — one success makes the next more likely, because removing a matched hat shrinks the pool — which is worth contrasting with the binomial of §4.8, where the trials were independent by construction. For $n=10$ the two numbers are $1/9\approx0.1111$ versus $1/10=0.1$; for $n=100$, $1/99\approx0.010101$ versus $0.01$.

    Blank 4 — $\mathbb{E}[X]$. Linearity of expectation (§4.6) needs no independence whatsoever, so it applies verbatim to these dependent indicators: $$\mathbb{E}[X]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^n X_i\Big]=\sum_{i=1}^n\mathbb{E}[X_i] =\underbrace{\tfrac1n+\tfrac1n+\cdots+\tfrac1n}_{n\text{ terms}}=n\cdot\tfrac1n=1 .$$ On average exactly one person gets their own hat, no matter how many people there are. Ten people, a hundred people, a million: the answer is $1$. Obtaining this from the PMF of $X$ instead would mean deriving the number of permutations with exactly $k$ fixed points — a far harder counting problem that the indicator method sidesteps completely.

    Blank 5 — $\mathbb{E}[X_i^2]$ (slide 8). $X_i$ takes only the values $0$ and $1$, and $0^2=0$, $1^2=1$, so $X_i^2=X_i$ as random variables. Hence $$\mathbb{E}[X_i^2]=\mathbb{E}[X_i]=\tfrac1n .$$

    Blank 6 — $\mathbf{P}(X_1X_2=1)$. The product $X_1X_2$ of two indicators is itself an indicator: it equals $1$ exactly when both $X_1=1$ and $X_2=1$. Apply the multiplication rule for events (G1 §2), which is where the dependence finally shows up: $$\mathbf{P}(X_1X_2=1)=\mathbf{P}(X_1=1,\ X_2=1)=\mathbf{P}(X_1=1)\,\mathbf{P}(X_2=1\mid X_1=1) =\frac1n\cdot\frac{1}{n-1}=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\qquad(n\ge2),$$ using Blank 1 for the first factor and Blank 3 for the second. Because $X_1X_2$ is an indicator, its expectation is that same probability: $$\mathbb{E}[X_iX_j]=\mathbf{P}(X_i=1,\ X_j=1)=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\qquad\text{for every }i\ne j,$$ the value being the same for every ordered pair by symmetry of the permutation. Note it is not $\mathbb{E}[X_i]\mathbb{E}[X_j]=1/n^2$ — that is precisely the independence assumption we do not have, and $\tfrac{1}{n(n-1)}>\tfrac{1}{n^2}$ records the positive dependence.

    Blank 7 — $\mathbb{E}[X^2]$. Square the decomposition and split the double sum into its diagonal ($i=j$) and off-diagonal ($i\ne j$) parts, exactly as slide 8 writes it: $$X^2=\Big(\sum_{i=1}^n X_i\Big)^2=\sum_{i=1}^n\sum_{j=1}^n X_iX_j =\sum_{i=1}^n X_i^2+\sum_{\substack{i,j\,:\,i\ne j}}X_iX_j .$$ Take expectations and use linearity once more (still no independence needed). The first sum has $n$ terms, each with expectation $1/n$ by Blank 5. The second sum runs over ordered pairs $(i,j)$ with $i\ne j$: there are $n$ choices for $i$ and then $n-1$ for $j$, so $n(n-1)$ terms, each with expectation $\frac{1}{n(n-1)}$ by Blank 6. Therefore $$\mathbb{E}[X^2]=\underbrace{n\cdot\frac1n}_{\text{diagonal}} +\underbrace{n(n-1)\cdot\frac{1}{n(n-1)}}_{\text{off-diagonal}}=1+1=2\qquad(n\ge2).$$ The two cancellations are the whole trick: the count of terms is exactly the reciprocal of each term's expectation, in both blocks.

    Blank 8 — $\operatorname{var}(X)$. Use the shortcut formula of §3.2 together with $\mathbb{E}[X]=1$ from Blank 4: $$\operatorname{var}(X)=\mathbb{E}[X^2]-(\mathbb{E}[X])^2=2-1^2=2-1=1\qquad(n\ge2).$$ So the number of people who get their own hat has mean $1$ and variance $1$, for every $n\ge2$. (For $n=1$ the single person always gets their own hat, $X\equiv1$, and $\operatorname{var}(X)=0$; there are no off-diagonal terms.)

    Numerical cross-check. Enumerating all $n!$ permutations and computing $X$ directly gives $\mathbb{E}[X]=1$, $\mathbb{E}[X^2]=2$, $\operatorname{var}(X)=1$ for $n=2,3,5$, and the closed forms give the same for $n=10$ and $n=100$ ($\mathbf{P}(X_1X_2=1)=1/90$ and $1/9900$ respectively) — see computes/g2_s4.py.

    Interpretation. Compare the two variance computations side by side. For the binomial (§4.8) the off-diagonal expectations were $\mathbb{E}[X_iX_j]=p^2=\mathbb{E}[X_i] \mathbb{E}[X_j]$, so all the cross terms cancelled against $(\mathbb{E}[X])^2$ and the variance collapsed to the sum $np(1-p)$ of individual variances. Here they do not cancel, and the entire extra slide of work is the price of that. What is striking is the answer: mean $1$ and variance $1$ independent of $n$, the signature of the Poisson distribution — and indeed the number of fixed points of a random permutation converges to Poisson with parameter $1$ as $n\to\infty$. The naive guess "$n$ people, so more matches for larger $n$" is wrong: more people also means each individual match is rarer, at exactly the compensating rate.
    Gotcha. Do not write $\operatorname{var}(X)=\sum_i\operatorname{var}(X_i) =n\cdot\tfrac1n\big(1-\tfrac1n\big)=1-\tfrac1n$. That is the independence formula, and the $X_i$ here are dependent; it gives $0.9$ for $n=10$ instead of the correct $1$. Whenever the indicators come from sampling without replacement — hats, cards, a fixed permutation, drawing balls from an urn — expect dependence and go through the $\mathbb{E}[X_iX_j]$ expansion. The mean, by contrast, is always safe.
    Practice 4.22. With $n=3$ people, write out the PMF of $X$ by listing all $3!=6$ permutations, and verify $\mathbb{E}[X]=1$ and $\operatorname{var}(X)=1$ directly.
    Solution

    Label the permutations by which hat each of persons $1,2,3$ receives. The six equally likely outcomes and their fixed-point counts are $$(1,2,3)\mapsto3,\quad(1,3,2)\mapsto1,\quad(2,1,3)\mapsto1,\quad(3,2,1)\mapsto1,\quad (2,3,1)\mapsto0,\quad(3,1,2)\mapsto0 .$$ So $p_X(0)=\tfrac26=\tfrac13$, $p_X(1)=\tfrac36=\tfrac12$, $p_X(2)=0$ (fixing two people forces the third), $p_X(3)=\tfrac16$; they sum to $\tfrac{2+3+0+1}{6}=1\ ✓$. Then $$\mathbb{E}[X]=0\cdot\tfrac13+1\cdot\tfrac12+3\cdot\tfrac16=\tfrac12+\tfrac12=1\ ✓,$$ $$\mathbb{E}[X^2]=0\cdot\tfrac13+1\cdot\tfrac12+9\cdot\tfrac16=\tfrac12+\tfrac32=2\ ✓,\qquad \operatorname{var}(X)=2-1=1\ ✓ .$$ Note $p_X(2)=0$: you can never have exactly $n-1$ fixed points.

    Practice 4.23. $n$ people draw hats as above. Let $Y$ be the number of ordered pairs $(i,j)$, $i\ne j$, in which both people get their own hat. Find $\mathbb{E}[Y]$, and reconcile it with $\mathbb{E}[X^2]=2$.
    Solution

    $Y=\sum_{i\ne j}X_iX_j$, a sum of $n(n-1)$ indicators each with expectation $\frac{1}{n(n-1)}$ (Blank 6). By linearity $\mathbb{E}[Y]=n(n-1)\cdot\frac{1}{n(n-1)}=1$ for every $n\ge2$. Reconciling: $X^2=\sum_i X_i^2+Y$, so $\mathbb{E}[X^2]=\mathbb{E}\big[\sum_i X_i^2\big]+\mathbb{E}[Y]=1+1=2\ ✓$ — $\mathbb{E}[Y]$ is the off-diagonal block of Blank 7. (In unordered terms, the expected number of matched pairs is $\binom{n}{2}\frac{1}{n(n-1)}=\tfrac12$.)

    Practice 4.24. Ten cards numbered $1,\dots,10$ are shuffled and dealt into ten numbered slots. Let $X$ count the slots receiving their matching card. Give $\mathbb{E}[X]$ and $\operatorname{var}(X)$, and account numerically for the gap between the correct variance and the $1-\tfrac1{10}=0.9$ that an independence assumption would predict.
    Solution

    This is the hat problem with $n=10$, so $\mathbb{E}[X]=1$ and $\operatorname{var}(X)=1$ — the physical relabeling (cards and slots instead of hats and people) changes nothing, because the assignment is still a uniformly random permutation.

    The independence formula fails because $\mathbf{P}(X_i=1,X_j=1)=\frac{1}{10\cdot9}=\frac{1}{90}$ exceeds $\mathbf{P}(X_i=1)\mathbf{P}(X_j=1)=\frac{1}{100}$: matches are positively correlated, since each match removes one card and one slot, raising every remaining match probability from $1/10$ to $1/9$. The $90$ positive cross terms contribute exactly $90(\tfrac1{90}-\tfrac1{100})=0.1$ of extra variance, restoring $0.9+0.1=1$.

    §4 cheat sheet.
    • Joint PMF. $p_{X,Y}(x,y)=\mathbf{P}(X=x,Y=y)\ge0$, $\sum_x\sum_y p_{X,Y}(x,y)=1$. It is a table; every question is a sum of cells possibly followed by a division (L07 slide 2; B&T §2.5).
    • Marginals. $p_X(x)=\sum_y p_{X,Y}(x,y)$ — sum the other variable away (total probability theorem). Fig. 4.1 example: $p_X=(\tfrac3{20},\tfrac8{20},\tfrac6{20},\tfrac3{20})$, $p_Y=(\tfrac1{20},\tfrac5{20},\tfrac9{20},\tfrac5{20})$.
    • Conditional PMF. $p_{X|Y}(x\mid y)=p_{X,Y}(x,y)/p_Y(y)$ for $p_Y(y)>0$: a row rescaled to sum to $1$. Multiplication rule $p_{X,Y}=p_X\,p_{Y|X}=p_Y\,p_{X|Y}$; chain rule $p_{X,Y,Z}=p_X\,p_{Y|X}\,p_{Z|X,Y}$ (L07 slides 2–3; B&T §2.6).
    • Conditioning on an event $A$. Cross out cells outside $A$, add the rest to get $\mathbf{P}(A)$, divide. Slide-3 example: $\mathbf{P}(X\le2,Y\ge3)=\tfrac9{20}$, conditional cells $\tfrac29,\tfrac49,\tfrac19,\tfrac29$, $\mathbb{E}[X\mid B]=\tfrac53$, $\operatorname{var}(X\mid B)=\tfrac29$ — and $X,Y$ become independent given $B$.
    • Independence. $p_{X,Y}(x,y)=p_X(x)p_Y(y)$ for all $x,y$ (L07 slide 3, red). Support must be a rectangle; rows must be proportional. The slide-3 grid fails at $(1,1)$: $0\ne\tfrac3{20}\cdot\tfrac1{20}$, so $X,Y$ are dependent — even though the single pair of events $\{X=2\},\{Y=4\}$ happens to be independent.
    • Expectations. $\mathbb{E}[g(X,Y)]=\sum_x\sum_y g(x,y)p_{X,Y}(x,y)$; $\mathbb{E}[aX+bY]=a\mathbb{E}[X]+b\mathbb{E}[Y]$ always; $\mathbb{E}[XY]=\mathbb{E}[X]\mathbb{E}[Y]$ only under independence. In general $\mathbb{E}[g(X,Y)]\ne g(\mathbb{E}[X],\mathbb{E}[Y])$ ($6.95$ vs $7.105$ on the slide-3 grid).
    • Variances. $\operatorname{var}(aX)=a^2\operatorname{var}(X)$, $\operatorname{var}(X+a)=\operatorname{var}(X)$; $\operatorname{var}(X+Y)=\operatorname{var}(X)+\operatorname{var}(Y)+2\operatorname{cov}(X,Y)$, so independence $\Rightarrow$ variances add. Degenerate cases: $Y=X$ gives $4\operatorname{var}(X)$; $Y=-X$ gives $0$; $Z=X-3Y$ with independence gives $\operatorname{var}(X)+9\operatorname{var}(Y)$ (L07 slide 5).
    • Indicators. $\mathbb{E}[\text{indicator}]=\mathbf{P}(\text{event})$. Binomial $X=\sum_i X_i$ gives $\mathbb{E}[X]=np$ (linearity only) and $\operatorname{var}(X)=np(1-p)$ (independence needed), replacing the brute-force sum on L07 slide 6.
    • Hat problem (L07 slides 7–8; §4.10). $n$ people, a uniformly random permutation of their hats, $X=$ number of own-hat matches. $\mathbf{P}(X_i=1)=\mathbb{E}[X_i]=\mathbb{E}[X_i^2]=\tfrac1n$; the $X_i$ are dependent, $\mathbf{P}(X_2=1\mid X_1=1)=\tfrac1{n-1}$. Linearity alone gives $\mathbb{E}[X]=n\cdot\tfrac1n=1$. For the variance, $\mathbb{E}[X_iX_j]=\tfrac{1}{n(n-1)}$ over $n(n-1)$ ordered pairs $i\ne j$, so $\mathbb{E}[X^2]=1+1=2$ and $\operatorname{var}(X)=2-1=1$ — both free of $n$ (for $n\ge2$).
    • Recitation results. rec06 P1: $p_{K|N}(\cdot\mid2)=(\tfrac14,\tfrac12,\tfrac14)$, $p_{N|K}(\cdot\mid2)=(\tfrac25,\tfrac35)$, $\mathbb{E}[K]=\tfrac34$. rec06 P2: $x=0$ maximizes $\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=x]$ at $2$; $y=3$ maximizes $\operatorname{var}(X\mid Y=y)$ at $4$; $p_R=(\tfrac38,\tfrac14,\tfrac14,\tfrac18)$; $\mathbb{E}[XY]=\tfrac{15}{4}$, $\mathbb{E}[XY\mid A]=5$. rec07 P2: $p_X(1)=\tfrac13$, $\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=1]=2$, no independent completion, $p_{X,Y}(2,2)=\tfrac13$, $p_{X,Y\mid B}(2,2)=\tfrac49$.

    §5   Synthesis: the discrete toolbox

    Sources: L05 slides 2–8 · L06 slides 2–8 · L07 slides 2–8 · rec05 P1–P4 · rec06 P1–P3 · rec07 P1–P3 · B&T §2.1–2.7. Every number on this page is recomputed in computes/g2_s5.py (closed forms are checked against brute-force summation over the PMF).

    Three lectures replaced outcomes by numbers. The replacement bought two things that G1 could not express: a single object, the PMF, that carries a whole table of probabilities at once, and two summary numbers, the mean and the variance, that compress that table into a location and a spread. Everything else in G2 — conditional PMFs, joint PMFs, independence, indicators, total expectation — is machinery for computing those two numbers without ever writing the full table down. This section puts the whole toolbox on one page, lists the traps that generate most of the wrong answers, and says precisely what breaks when the values stop being discrete.

    5.1 How the pieces fit together

    Read the map below as a dependency graph: an arrow means "the target is defined in terms of, or derived from, the source". Three chains descend from the PMF. The left chain is conditioning — a conditional PMF is just a PMF on a smaller universe, and summing conditional expectations against the weights of a partition is the total expectation theorem. The middle chain is averaging — expectation, then the expected-value rule that lets you average any function of $X$ without finding its PMF, then variance as the special case $g(x)=(x-\mathbb{E}[X])^2$. The right chain is multiplicity — joint PMFs, the independence test, and the linearity/indicator machinery that makes sums tractable.

    Fig. 5.1 — Dependency map of the G2 toolbox (L05 slides 2–8; L06 slides 5, 7, 8; L07 slides 3–7). Solid arrows: definition or derivation. The bottom box is the G3 preview of §5.5.
    Interpretation — there are only three moves. The first is partition and add: $p_X(x)$ itself is built by collecting all outcomes with $X(\omega)=x$ and adding their probabilities (L05 slide 4), marginalization $p_X(x)=\sum_y p_{X,Y}(x,y)$ adds over a partition by the value of $Y$ (L06 slide 8), and total expectation adds conditional means over a partition of $\Omega$ (L06 slide 7). The second is reweight without rebuilding: the expected-value rule $\mathbb{E}[g(X)]=\sum_x g(x)p_X(x)$ says you may leave the PMF of $X$ alone and change only the numbers you multiply it by (L05 slide 7) — that single trick delivers second moments, variances, and every "average cost/time/profit" question in the recitations. The third is decompose into indicators: write $X=\sum_i X_i$ with each $X_i\in\{0,1\}$ and use linearity (L07 slides 6–7). If a G2 problem feels hard, you are almost certainly missing one of these three moves.
    Practice 5.1 (name the move). For each task, say which of the three moves — partition and add, reweight without rebuilding, decompose into indicators — does the work, and write the one identity you would invoke.
    1. You have $p_X$ for a die-like $X$ and want $\mathbb{E}[3X^2-2X+1]$.
    2. $400$ students have independent uniform birthdays; you want the expected number of days of the year on which at least one birthday falls.
    3. A fair coin decides whether $X$ is uniform on $\{1,\ldots,4\}$ or geometric($1/5$); you want $\mathbb{E}[X]$.
    4. You have $p_{X,Y}$ and want $p_Y$.
    Solution

    1. Reweight without rebuilding. The expected-value rule $\mathbb{E}[g(X)]=\sum_x g(x)p_X(x)$ with $g(x)=3x^2-2x+1$ (L05 slide 7). You never need the PMF of $3X^2-2X+1$. Linearity then lets you split it further as $3\mathbb{E}[X^2]-2\mathbb{E}[X]+1$ if you prefer.

    2. Decompose into indicators. Let $D_d=1$ if day $d$ has at least one birthday. Then the count is $\sum_{d=1}^{365}D_d$ and $\mathbb{E}[\text{count}]=\sum_d\mathbf{P}(D_d=1)$ (L07 slides 6–7). The $D_d$ are dependent — there are only $400$ birthdays to go around — and linearity does not care.

    3. Partition and add. Total expectation over the partition $\{$heads, tails$\}$: $\mathbb{E}[X]=\tfrac12\mathbb{E}[X\mid\text{heads}]+\tfrac12\mathbb{E}[X\mid\text{tails}]$ (L06 slide 7).

    4. Partition and add. Marginalization $p_Y(y)=\sum_x p_{X,Y}(x,y)$ partitions the event $\{Y=y\}$ by the value of $X$ (L06 slide 8).

    Practice 5.2 (the middle chain, end to end). $X$ is the number of heads in three tosses of a fair coin. Using only the expected-value rule and the computational form of the variance, find $\mathbb{E}[X]$, $\mathbb{E}[X^2]$ and $\operatorname{var}(X)$, then check the last against the binomial formula in §5.2.
    Solution

    $X\sim$ binomial($3,\tfrac12$), so $p_X(0)=p_X(3)=\tfrac18$ and $p_X(1)=p_X(2)=\tfrac38$ (these sum to $1$). The definition gives $$\mathbb{E}[X]=0\cdot\tfrac18+1\cdot\tfrac38+2\cdot\tfrac38+3\cdot\tfrac18=\tfrac{0+3+6+3}{8}=\tfrac{12}{8}=\tfrac32 .$$ The expected-value rule with $g(x)=x^2$ reuses that same PMF — no new PMF is built: $$\mathbb{E}[X^2]=0\cdot\tfrac18+1\cdot\tfrac38+4\cdot\tfrac38+9\cdot\tfrac18=\tfrac{0+3+12+9}{8}=\tfrac{24}{8}=3 .$$ Then $\operatorname{var}(X)=\mathbb{E}[X^2]-(\mathbb{E}[X])^2=3-\tfrac94=\tfrac34=0.75$, and the table's $np(1-p)=3\cdot\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac12=\tfrac34$ agrees. (Compute key practice.p5_1b.)

    5.2 The PMF zoo

    Four families cover almost every discrete model in this course. In the table, $p$ is the success probability of a single trial, $n$ is the number of trials, and $a,b$ are the smallest and largest values of the uniform range (all integers between them are included). The "mechanism" column is the sentence in the problem statement that should make you reach for that row.

    FamilyPMF$\mathbb{E}[X]$$\operatorname{var}(X)$Mechanism that produces itSource
    Bernoulli($p$) $p_X(1)=p$, $p_X(0)=1-p$ $p$ $p(1-p)$ One trial with two outcomes; an indicator of any event $A$ with $p=\mathbf{P}(A)$. L07 s6, s7; B&T §2.2
    Uniform on $\{a,\ldots,b\}$ $p_X(k)=\dfrac{1}{b-a+1}$, $k=a,\ldots,b$ $\dfrac{a+b}{2}$ $\dfrac{(b-a)(b-a+2)}{12}$ "Equally likely", "fair die", "picked at random" with no value preferred over another. L05 s6; L06 s5; B&T §2.2
    Binomial($n,p$) $p_X(k)=\dbinom{n}{k}p^k(1-p)^{n-k}$, $k=0,\ldots,n$ $np$ $np(1-p)$ Count the successes in a fixed number $n$ of independent identical trials. L05 s5; L07 s6; rec05 P2; B&T §2.2
    Geometric($p$) $p_X(k)=(1-p)^{k-1}p$, $k=1,2,\ldots$ $\dfrac{1}{p}$ $\dfrac{1-p}{p^2}$ Wait for the first success in independent identical trials; the number of trials is unbounded. L05 s3; L06 s6, s7; rec06 P3; B&T §2.4

    The special value $a=0$, $b=n$ of the uniform row is the one on L05 slide 6: mean $n/2$ and variance $n(n+2)/12$, both verified in computes/g2_s5.py for $n=1,4,9,100$ against brute-force summation (key uniform_0_n_rule.instances: $n=1\to\tfrac12,\tfrac14$; $n=4\to 2,2$; $n=9\to\tfrac92,\tfrac{33}{4}$; $n=100\to 50, 850$). Three sanity instances of the other rows, each computed twice — once from the closed form, once by summing over the PMF:

    Instance$\sum_x p_X(x)$$\mathbb{E}[X]$$\mathbb{E}[X^2]$$\operatorname{var}(X)$
    Bernoulli($0.3$)$1$$0.3$$0.3$$0.21$
    Binomial($10,0.3$)$1$$3$$11.1$$2.1$
    Geometric($0.2$), summed to $k=4000$$1.000000000000000$$5.000000000000$—$20.000000000000$
    Interpretation — the zoo is two mechanisms, not four. Bernoulli is the atom. Binomial and geometric are the two different questions you can ask about a stream of Bernoulli trials: fix the number of trials and count the successes (binomial), or fix the number of successes at one and count the trials (geometric). That is why their means point in opposite directions: more trials raises $np$, whereas a larger success probability lowers $1/p$. The uniform is the odd one out — it encodes ignorance rather than a trial mechanism, and it is the only one of the four whose variance does not involve $p$ at all.
    Interpretation — where the variances come from. $p(1-p)$ is maximized at $p=1/2$ (value $0.25$) and vanishes at $p=0$ and $p=1$: a coin you already know the answer to has no spread. The binomial variance is $n$ copies of that, $np(1-p)$, because a binomial random variable is a sum of $n$ independent indicators and variances of independent random variables add (L07 slide 5). The geometric variance $(1-p)/p^2$ blows up as $p\to 0$ far faster than its mean $1/p$ does — for $p=0.2$ the mean is $5$ but the standard deviation is $\sqrt{20}\approx 4.47$, i.e. almost as large as the mean itself. Rare-event waiting times are wildly unpredictable, not merely long.
    Recipe — picking the tool.
    1. Is the quantity a number attached to each outcome? If not, it is an event and G1 already handles it. If yes, name it $X$ and say in words what $X(\omega)$ is.
    2. Does it come from one of the four mechanisms? one trial $\to$ Bernoulli; nothing prefers one value $\to$ uniform; count successes in $n$ fixed trials $\to$ binomial; wait for the first success $\to$ geometric. Then read the mean and variance off the table and stop.
    3. If no family fits, build the PMF (L05 slide 4): collect the outcomes with $X(\omega)=x$, add their probabilities, repeat for every $x$, then check $\sum_x p_X(x)=1$.
    4. Then answer the actual question. A mean of a function of $X$ $\to$ the expected-value rule, never a new PMF. A mean of a sum $\to$ linearity and indicators, and note that independence is not required. A spread $\to$ $\operatorname{var}(X)=\mathbb{E}[X^2]-(\mathbb{E}[X])^2$. A mean you can only compute inside each scenario $\to$ total expectation.
    Fig. 5.2 — Decision flowchart for a discrete problem (L05 slides 3–8; L06 slides 5–7; L07 slides 4–7).
    Practice 5.3 (identify the family). For each verbal setup, name the family and its parameters, then read off $\mathbb{E}[X]$ and $\operatorname{var}(X)$ from the table above.
    1. A machine stamps out parts; each is defective with probability $0.02$, independently of the others. $X$ = number of defectives in a batch of $50$.
    2. You half-remember a phone number and try a guess; each attempt is correct with probability $1/4$, independently. $X$ = number of attempts up to and including the first correct one.
    3. A fair $12$-sided die is rolled once and $X$ is the face shown.
    4. One student is picked at random from a class of $30$ that contains $12$ women; $X=1$ if the student picked is a woman and $X=0$ otherwise.
    Solution

    1. Binomial($50,0.02$) — the mechanism is "count the successes in a fixed number of independent identical trials". $\mathbb{E}[X]=np=50(0.02)=1$ and $\operatorname{var}(X)=np(1-p)=50(0.02)(0.98)=0.98$.

    2. Geometric($1/4$) — "wait for the first success", with the number of trials unbounded. $\mathbb{E}[X]=1/p=4$ and $\operatorname{var}(X)=(1-p)/p^2=(3/4)/(1/16)=12$. Note the standard deviation $\sqrt{12}\approx 3.46$ is nearly as big as the mean, as always for a geometric.

    3. Uniform on $\{1,\ldots,12\}$ — "fair die", nothing prefers one value. With $a=1,b=12$, $\mathbb{E}[X]=(a+b)/2=13/2=6.5$ and $\operatorname{var}(X)=(b-a)(b-a+2)/12=(11)(13)/12=143/12\approx 11.916667$.

    4. Bernoulli($0.4$) — one trial with two outcomes; $X$ is the indicator of the event "a woman is picked", whose probability is $12/30=0.4$. So $\mathbb{E}[X]=p=0.4$ and $\operatorname{var}(X)=p(1-p)=0.4(0.6)=0.24$. (All four in compute key practice.p5_2a.)

    Practice 5.4 (when no family fits). Five cards are dealt from a well-shuffled $52$-card deck, without replacement. Let $X$ be the number of aces among them. Is $X$ binomial? What is $\mathbb{E}[X]$?
    Solution

    Not binomial. Step 2 of the recipe fails: the five draws are trials with two outcomes, but they are not independent — drawing an ace first lowers the chance of an ace second, from $4/52$ to $3/51$. The true PMF is hypergeometric.

    The mean is still easy, because linearity needs no independence. Let $X_i=1$ if the $i$th card dealt is an ace. By symmetry each single card is equally likely to be any of the $52$, so $\mathbf{P}(X_i=1)=4/52=1/13$ for every $i$ — including the fifth. Then $$\mathbb{E}[X]=\sum_{i=1}^{5}\mathbf{P}(X_i=1)=5\cdot\frac{1}{13}=\frac{5}{13}\approx 0.384615,$$ exactly what binomial($5,1/13$) would have said. The variance is where the difference shows up: the true value is $940/2873\approx 0.327184$, smaller than the binomial answer $5\cdot\frac1{13}\cdot\frac{12}{13}\approx 0.355030$, because sampling without replacement makes the indicators negatively dependent. This is the §5.3 fault line in miniature: means transfer, variances of sums do not. (Compute key practice.p5_2b, checked against the exact hypergeometric PMF.)

    5.3 The expectation and variance identities

    Every identity of G2 in one place. Here $X,Y$ are random variables on the same sample space, $g$ and $h$ are real-valued functions, $a,b,\alpha,\beta$ are constants (not random), $\{A_i\}$ is a partition of $\Omega$ into disjoint events with $\mathbf{P}(A_i)>0$, and $\sigma_X$ is the standard deviation. The "needs independence" column is the one to check first when an identity fails.

    IdentityStatementNeeds independence?Source
    Expectation
    Definition$\mathbb{E}[X]=\sum_x x\,p_X(x)$ (center of gravity of the PMF)noL05 s6; B&T §2.4
    Expected-value rule$\mathbb{E}[g(X)]=\sum_x g(x)\,p_X(x)$ — no need for $p_{g(X)}$noL05 s7; rec05 P1(a)
    Two-variable version$\mathbb{E}[g(X,Y)]=\sum_x\sum_y g(x,y)\,p_{X,Y}(x,y)$noL07 s4; rec07 P1
    Constants$\mathbb{E}[\alpha]=\alpha$noL05 s7
    Scaling / shifting$\mathbb{E}[\alpha X+\beta]=\alpha\mathbb{E}[X]+\beta$noL05 s7; rec05 P1(b)
    Centering$\mathbb{E}\big[X-\mathbb{E}[X]\big]=0$noL06 s2
    Linearity (sums)$\mathbb{E}[aX+bY]=a\mathbb{E}[X]+b\mathbb{E}[Y]$; $\mathbb{E}[X_1+\cdots+X_n]=\sum_i\mathbb{E}[X_i]$no — the most useful fact in G2L07 s4; rec07 P1
    Products$\mathbb{E}[XY]=\mathbb{E}[X]\,\mathbb{E}[Y]$, and $\mathbb{E}[g(X)h(Y)]=\mathbb{E}[g(X)]\,\mathbb{E}[h(Y)]$yesL07 s4
    Total expectation$\mathbb{E}[X]=\sum_i \mathbf{P}(A_i)\,\mathbb{E}[X\mid A_i]$, with $\mathbb{E}[X\mid A_i]=\sum_x x\,p_{X\mid A_i}(x)$noL06 s5, s7; rec07 P3
    Indicator method$X=\sum_i X_i$, $X_i\in\{0,1\}$ $\Rightarrow$ $\mathbb{E}[X]=\sum_i\mathbf{P}(X_i=1)$noL07 s6, s7
    Variance
    Definition$\operatorname{var}(X)=\mathbb{E}\big[(X-\mathbb{E}[X])^2\big]=\sum_x (x-\mathbb{E}[X])^2p_X(x)\ \ge 0$noL05 s8
    Computational form$\operatorname{var}(X)=\mathbb{E}[X^2]-(\mathbb{E}[X])^2$noL05 s8; rec05 P1(c)
    Standard deviation$\sigma_X=\sqrt{\operatorname{var}(X)}$ — same units as $X$noL06 s2
    Shift invariance$\operatorname{var}(X+a)=\operatorname{var}(X)$noL07 s5
    Scaling$\operatorname{var}(aX+b)=a^2\operatorname{var}(X)$, so $\sigma_{aX+b}=|a|\,\sigma_X$noL05 s8; L07 s5
    Sums$\operatorname{var}(X+Y)=\operatorname{var}(X)+\operatorname{var}(Y)$yesL07 s5
    Linear combination$X,Y$ independent $\Rightarrow$ $\operatorname{var}(aX+bY)=a^2\operatorname{var}(X)+b^2\operatorname{var}(Y)$yesL07 s5
    PMF bookkeeping
    Normalization$\sum_x p_X(x)=1$; $\sum_x\sum_y p_{X,Y}(x,y)=1$; $\sum_x p_{X\mid Y}(x\mid y)=1$ for each fixed $y$noL05 s3; L06 s8
    Marginalization$p_X(x)=\sum_y p_{X,Y}(x,y)$noL06 s8; L07 s2
    Multiplication rule$p_{X,Y}(x,y)=p_X(x)\,p_{Y\mid X}(y\mid x)$; chain rule for three: $p_{X,Y,Z}=p_X\,p_{Y\mid X}\,p_{Z\mid X,Y}$noL07 s2, s3
    Total probability for PMFs$p_X(x)=\sum_i\mathbf{P}(A_i)\,p_{X\mid A_i}(x)$noL06 s7
    Independence$p_{X,Y}(x,y)=p_X(x)p_Y(y)$ for all $x,y$ (three r.v.'s: the triple product for all $x,y,z$)—L07 s3
    MemorylessnessGeometric: given $X>n$, the r.v. $X-n$ is again geometric($p$)—L06 s6
    Interpretation — the fault line runs through the middle of the table. Notice that every expectation identity except the product rule holds without any independence assumption, while every variance-of-a-sum identity needs it. That asymmetry is the single most exploitable fact in the subject. It is why the hat problem (L07 slides 7–8) can compute $\mathbb{E}[X]=1$ in one line by linearity even though the indicators $X_i$ are strongly dependent, and it is why computing $\operatorname{var}(X)=1$ in that same problem took a whole extra slide of work with the cross terms $\mathbb{E}[X_iX_j]$ (worked in full in Example 5.1 below). Whenever a problem asks for a mean of a sum, look for indicators before you look for a PMF.
    Example 5.1 (L07 slides 7–8 — the hat problem, worked in full). $n\ge 2$ people throw their hats into a box, the hats are mixed, and each person then draws one hat at random — formally, the assignment of hats to people is a uniformly random permutation of the $n$ hats. Let $X$ be the number of people who get their own hat back. Find $\mathbb{E}[X]$ and $\operatorname{var}(X)$. Both slides leave every step blank; the derivation below fills them, and every value is recomputed in computes/g2_s5.py (keys hat, hat_ingredients) at $n=3,5,10,100$.

    Step 1 — decompose into indicators. Writing the PMF of $X$ directly is a derangement count and is genuinely hard; the indicator method avoids it entirely. For $i=1,\ldots,n$ set $$X_i=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if person }i\text{ draws his own hat},\\ 0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases} \qquad\text{so that}\qquad X=X_1+X_2+\cdots+X_n,$$ because adding a $1$ for each person who succeeds is exactly counting the successes.

    Step 2 — the single-indicator probability. Person $i$ is equally likely to end up with any one of the $n$ hats (symmetry: the permutation is uniform, so every hat is equally likely to land on person $i$). Exactly one of those $n$ hats is his own, so $$\mathbf{P}(X_i=1)=\frac{1}{n},\qquad\text{hence}\qquad \mathbb{E}[X_i]=1\cdot\frac1n+0\cdot\Big(1-\frac1n\Big)=\frac1n,$$ using the definition of expectation on a two-value PMF. (At $n=3$ this is $1/3$; at $n=100$ it is $1/100$.)

    Step 3 — the mean, by linearity alone. The $X_i$ are not independent: given that person $1$ took his own hat, person $2$ now draws from $n-1$ hats, so $\mathbf{P}(X_2=1\mid X_1=1)=1/(n-1)\neq 1/n$. Linearity does not care, since it holds for any random variables on the same sample space (L07 slide 4): $$\mathbb{E}[X]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[X_i]=n\cdot\frac1n=1.$$ On average exactly one person gets his own hat, whatever $n$ is.

    Step 4 — expand the square. Use the computational form $\operatorname{var}(X)=\mathbb{E}[X^2]-(\mathbb{E}[X])^2=\mathbb{E}[X^2]-1$ (L05 slide 8, with $\mathbb{E}[X]=1$ from step 3). Multiplying out the sum and splitting the $n^2$ products into the $n$ with $i=j$ and the rest, $$X^2=\Big(\sum_i X_i\Big)^2=\sum_{i}X_i^2+\sum_{i\neq j}X_iX_j .$$

    Step 5 — the diagonal terms. Each $X_i$ takes only the values $0$ and $1$, and $0^2=0$, $1^2=1$, so $X_i^2=X_i$ identically. Hence $\mathbb{E}[X_i^2]=\mathbb{E}[X_i]=1/n$, and the $n$ diagonal terms contribute $$\sum_{i}\mathbb{E}[X_i^2]=n\cdot\frac1n=1 .$$

    Step 6 — the cross terms. For $i\neq j$ the product $X_iX_j$ is again a $0$–$1$ variable: it equals $1$ exactly when persons $i$ and $j$ both get their own hats. So its expectation is that event's probability, and the multiplication rule of G1 gives $$\mathbb{E}[X_iX_j]=\mathbf{P}(X_i=1,\,X_j=1)=\mathbf{P}(X_i=1)\,\mathbf{P}(X_j=1\mid X_i=1) =\frac1n\cdot\frac{1}{n-1}=\frac{1}{n(n-1)},$$ the conditional factor being $1/(n-1)$ because once person $i$ has removed his own hat, person $j$ is equally likely to hold any of the remaining $n-1$ hats, one of which is his. Note this is larger than $\frac1n\cdot\frac1n$, the value independence would have predicted.

    Step 7 — count the cross terms and add. The index pairs $(i,j)$ with $i\neq j$ are ordered: $n$ choices for $i$ and $n-1$ remaining choices for $j$, so there are $n(n-1)$ of them. Every one has the same expectation, so by linearity $$\mathbb{E}[X^2]=\underbrace{n\cdot\frac1n}_{\text{diagonal}} +\underbrace{n(n-1)\cdot\frac{1}{n(n-1)}}_{\text{cross terms}}=1+1=2 .$$

    Step 8 — the variance. $$\operatorname{var}(X)=\mathbb{E}[X^2]-(\mathbb{E}[X])^2=2-1^2=1 .$$ Both the mean and the variance are $1$ for every $n\ge 2$ — the count of matches neither grows nor spreads as the crowd gets larger. Each factor of $n$ in a count is cancelled by a factor of $1/n$ in a probability, twice over in step 7.

    Why this needed a whole extra slide. Step 3 was one line because linearity is free. Steps 4–7 were long because $\operatorname{var}(X)=\sum_i\operatorname{var}(X_i)$ is illegal here: the $X_i$ are dependent, and that rule would have given $n\cdot\frac1n(1-\frac1n)=1-\frac1n$, which is wrong for every finite $n$ (it says $2/3$ at $n=3$). The cross terms are exactly the correction that the illegal shortcut throws away.

    The following worked values are the calibration points of G2. If you can reproduce all of them you have the section (all recomputed in computes/g2_s5.py).
